## The principle: what the user decides wins

The owner, 2026-10-06: "For B-4, I think what the user decides should always override the automatics." Every rule below follows from it.

- **A start by hand replaces an automatic session.** Any start that the user (or a program in the user's name) asks for, with a length, an end time, `--indefinite`, `-w`, `--`, the picker or the app, ends the automatic session and starts exactly the session asked for (the handover, 5.9). It does not add time to the automatic session, and it is not refused, also when the picker or the terminal prompt was open while an automatic session started or ended. If the new session cannot start after all, a closed Mac still sleeps, as the automatic session would have let it (5.9). `--if-off` (the app's keyboard shortcut and `Start default session`) still changes nothing while a session the user started runs, as it promises (7234-7243), but it counts an automatic session as off and replaces it, as the app's last poll may have been made before the automatic session began.
- **Added time is a start by hand.** A time option during an automatic session gives the user's own session with that end, not "the automatic session plus some time", and the CLI says so. Like every session the user starts by time, it is a minimum while AI works (below): it lasts the hour, or longer while AI still works. The app's menu offers `Keep awake for 1 hour` (the length to add from Settings) instead of `Add 1 hour` during an automatic session (M4). The owner, 2026-10-06: "For B-14, it makes sense for me that added time works as proposed."
- **A stop wins, for every agent.** A stop ends the session and pauses automatic sessions for all agents until the user's own next prompt, typed at the Mac after the stop (B-5). `Resume now` in the menu lifts the pause at once. The pause is written by the stop itself, before the session ends, so no automatic start can slip in between (5.9), and it survives a restart (W10).
- **A setting change wins.** Turning the feature off, for all agents or for one, takes effect at once: the hooks go, and an automatic session that only that agent kept ends, as `process_exited`, so that a closed Mac sleeps (K11). A new lid mode or new guardrails reach the running automatic session within 30 s: with the lid open the watcher replaces it with one that has the new settings, and with the lid closed a switch to lid-open lets the Mac sleep (5.9). A session the user started keeps the settings it started with, as today (7280-7287).
- **The automatics never shorten, end or replace the user's own session, and a timed one is a minimum.** The owner, 2026-10-06: "If the user has started an awake session for 10 minutes and then puts an AI agent to work which takes 2 hours, then does the session stop after 10 minutes or after 2 hours? For me stopping the session in this case after 2 hours sounds logical." So while the feature is on, a session the user started by time (a length or an end time) goes on past its end while AI works (the floor, 5.9, W30). It ends at the later of its own end and 2 minutes after its last renewal; the renewals run only from 90 s before its own end and only while AI works, so when the work ends earlier the session ends at its own end, and otherwise 90 to 120 s after the work's end. The watcher only ever moves its end later, through the helper's `extend` with `@EPOCH`, which never moves an end earlier (awake-helper:1128-1141). They start nothing while it runs. In detail:
  - `--indefinite` has no end to keep; a session tied to a process (`-w`, `--`) ends with its process, as the user chose, even while AI works (5.9);
  - a lid-closed session is kept only in password-free mode, as a hook must never ask for a password; without it, it ends at its own end, and with the lid closed the Mac then sleeps (awake-helper:850-855);
  - the user's explicit actions still win: a stop ends it at once and pauses the automatics; before its own end, added time adds to its current end as today, which in its last 90 s the floor may already have moved up to 2 minutes from now, and past its own end a start by hand replaces it (B-16); turning the feature off stops the renewals, so it ends at its own end, or at its last renewed end, at most 2 minutes away, when the floor had already renewed it;
  - when it ends with the lid open while an agent still works (a session tied to a process, or one the floor could not keep), an automatic session may follow within a pass (2 s); with the lid closed, no automatic session starts until the lid is open (B-11).
- **The user's own session never lifts a pause.** A start by hand, also one an agent runs through its shell tool, runs as asked, but it clears neither a stop pause nor a guardrail pause nor the presence limit; only the user's typed prompt (for the presence limit, any input at the Mac), `Resume now` or the cause going away does (5.9, 5.10).
- **The guardrails are the user's settings too.** They end automatic sessions as any session, and automatic sessions then pause until the cause has gone (5.10).
- **What the automatics may do:** start a session only when none runs, no pause stands, the presence limit is not reached, the watcher has read how the last one ended, and the lid is open (or the Mac is in closed-display mode, B-11); move the end of their own session, or of the user's timed session within its last 90 s and past it (the floor), later and never earlier; and end only their own session. Each renewal and each end is checked by token under the main lock (W6, W29).
- **Every automatic session ends by itself, whatever fails.** The owner, 2026-10-06: "Since the cap is 365 days, it is crucial that automatic sessions stop in any imaginable situation." So the end does not rest on the watcher's health: an automatic session is a timed session with a 2-minute end that the watcher keeps renewing while work goes on (the lease, B-12, W29). When nothing renews it, for whatever reason, the root helper's own timer ends it within 2 minutes, as it ends any timed session, and with the lid closed the Mac sleeps. Each renewal and each end runs as its own short process under a time limit, so one that hangs is killed and leaves no lock behind that could hold up the end (W29). Hooks that fall silent end their holders by the time rules first, and the watcher renews nothing 2 hours after the last hook event in any case, with event times that a clock set back cannot move into the future (W3). An agent that never stops, such as a loop whose turns follow each other within the grace, keeps renewing rightly; the presence limit ends that too, as nothing is renewed once nobody has used the Mac for 24 hours (B-18, recommended). 5.14 lists every failure and its worst case. The 365 days stay the outer limit (W29).

## Questions for the owner

The owner answered questions in two rounds on 2026-10-06; they are marked "Answered". The open ones follow their recommended answer until the owner decides. The section named in each question explains it.

| Question | Answer on 2026-10-06 |
|---|---|
| S-1, a setting or a one-off choice | In effect (a), a setting: "the idea appears to be making 'AI runs -> awake on' and 'AI stops -> awake off' triggers automatic". Say so if (c) is wanted. |
| S-2, which agents | "Awake should offer this functionality for all the current mainstream AIs such as Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, and Gemini. I currently use Cursor as my editor and run Claude Extension inside it." S-4 now asks which surfaces of them. |
| B-1 and B-2, the timings | "I think the timings are appropriate": 60 s after the work ends, 10 minutes of waiting for the user, the 15-minute idle limit, 1 hour of background work. In the second round: the timings stay as they are. |
| B-2, the session limit | "I would still put the cap in 365, then it is the same as in other use. I guess in the future, it could be possible to have really long automatic AI runs." B-12 asks what then bounds an automatic session. |
| B-4, a session started by hand | "What the user decides should always override the automatics": (a), and the principle above. Refined in the second round: a session the user started by time is a minimum while AI works (B-4 (d)). |
| B-5, a stop by the user | Asked as a question. Answered below with a recommendation, for the owner to confirm. |
| Second round: the agents' facts | "You said that Cursor's site and the Codex and Gemini docs sites were blocked from there. Does adrafinil code in https://github.com/kageroumado/adrafinil give any hint how they work?" It does, and so do the Codex and Gemini CLI repositories on GitHub: section 2 now says where each Cursor, Codex and Gemini fact rests, and what they corrected. |
| S-3, names | "I think naming this property 'AI' makes sense": (a). |
| S-4, which agents and surfaces | "It is important to include only those AI agents that can be made work with awake": the inclusion rule. |
| B-12, what ends an automatic session | "Since the cap is 365 days, it is crucial that automatic sessions stop in any imaginable situation": the lease, (a). |
| B-14, added time during an automatic session | "For B-14, it makes sense for me that added time works as proposed": (a), which with the floor lasts the hour or longer while AI works. |

H-1, H-3 to H-6, B-5 and B-13 shape the most code among the open questions. B-15 (the lease's length), B-16 (a start by hand during the floor) and B-17 (when a floored session ends), new in the third revision, change constants or a few branches. B-18 (what ends an agent that never stops) and B-19 (a timed start by hand during a stop pause) came from the reviews of the third revision; B-18 is the one open question about the owner's "stop in any imaginable situation".

### Scope and names

- **S-1. What does "keep awake while AI works" mean in Awake? (sections 4, 5)** Answered on 2026-10-06, in effect: (a).
  - (a) A setting. Once it is on, every turn of a connected agent keeps the Mac awake, also with the lid closed, and the Mac sleeps again about a minute after the work stops. A checkmark item in the Ctrl-click menu turns it on and off. This is Adrafinil's model, and it matches how agents are used: prompt after prompt, and sometimes with the lid closed. **Recommended, and the owner's answer.**
  - (b) A one-off choice in the picker's `Custom…` step, `While AI works`, next to `For`, `Until` and `While`. It starts a session now and ends it the first time the agents stop working. The next turn starts nothing. It needs the same hooks as (a), and also a new end token and the picker's code (tools/awake-gui-picker.swift, around :938).
  - (c) Both. (a)'s code, plus (b)'s picker work and a "one-off" marker the watcher clears when it ends the session.

- **S-2. Which agents? (sections 1, 2)** Answered on 2026-10-06: all current mainstream agents, Claude Code, Codex, Cursor and Gemini, with the owner's own setup (Cursor as the editor, the Claude Code extension inside it, and Cursor's own agent) as the first worked example (section 6). The hook verbs, the holder keys and the event log were already not tied to Claude Code; the revision adds one adapter per agent (5.2, K.3). S-4 asks which surfaces.

- **S-3. Names, and where the switch lives. (sections 8, 9)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I think naming this property 'AI' makes sense."
  - (a) "AI". The menu item `Keep awake while AI works`, with a checkmark, shown when any supported agent is found on the Mac. The CLI: `awake --ai-hooks on|off|refresh|status|print|resume`, with `--agent claude|codex|cursor|gemini|all` (all agents found, by default) and `--config-dir DIR` for an agent's extra settings folder. A README section `While AI works`. A Settings group `AI agents`, with one row per agent, a lid-mode popup and the pause. The status names the agents at work: `Awake is on while Claude works.`, `Awake is on while Claude and Cursor work.` "AI" is the owner's own word, short, and clear to users who do not think of their assistant as an "agent". It also keeps the user's option apart from the internal `--agent-hook`. **Recommended, and the owner's answer.**
  - (b) "Agents": `Keep awake while agents work`, `--agent-hooks`. The usual technical word, but less plain, and one letter from the internal `--agent-hook`.
  - (c) One option per agent: `--claude-hooks`, `--codex-hooks`, `--cursor-hooks`, `--gemini-hooks`, each with the same verbs, and one menu item per agent. Each reads plainly, but the help grows by four options, each new agent adds another, the lid mode they share would be set in four places, and the owner's setup needs two of them.
  - (d) "Coding agents": `Keep awake while coding agents work`. Precise, and longer in a menu.

- **S-4. Which agents and surfaces in the first release? (2.1, K.3)** Answered on 2026-10-06. The owner: "It is important to include only those AI agents that can be made work with awake." That is the **inclusion rule**: an agent or a surface is offered only when both hold:
  1. the signals Awake uses there are documented by the vendor or read in the agent's own source code; and
  2. the Mac preflight (section 10) shows them working end to end with Awake on that surface, at a version the README names.
  Anything inferred or unverified, such as a closed-source surface whose behaviour is inferred from open code or an event no one has seen fire, is not offered until it is. The README and `--ai-hooks status` say which agents and surfaces are supported, at which version they were checked, and which are not, with the manual path for each (2.1, W.7). Adrafinil follows the same rule: "Only ship integrations whose format is device-verified" (adrafinil commit efb124c). Where one hook file serves several surfaces (Claude Code's `settings.json`, Codex's `hooks.json`), Awake cannot leave one surface out, as its hooks run wherever that agent reads the file; there "not offered" means that the README lists the surface as not verified and promises nothing for it.
  - (a) The four the owner named, on each surface that passes the rule. With the sources read for this revision, these can pass once their preflight step does (2.1):
    - Claude Code, all through the same `~/.claude/settings.json` (hooks.md:13; vs-code.md:525; desktop.md:906), each surface after its own step: a terminal such as Terminal or iTerm (10.3 (a)-(f)); headless `claude -p` (10.3 (h)); the integrated terminal of Cursor (10.2 (z)), and of VS Code or a JetBrains IDE once 10.3 has run in it; the extension in Cursor (10.2 (a)-(m)) and in VS Code (10.2 (w)); the Desktop app's local sessions (10.3 (g)). Programs that host Claude Code through the Agent SDK with the user's settings, such as Zed's Claude agent over ACP (2.6), run the same handlers, but no step runs them, so the README lists them as not verified;
    - Codex: the terminal and `codex exec`, through `~/.codex/hooks.json` once the user has trusted the hooks (H-3); preflight 10.4. Its IDE extension and desktop app are closed source and are not offered until 10.4 shows Awake's trusted hooks firing there;
    - Cursor's own agent, through `~/.cursor/hooks.json`: Agent chat (10.2 (n)-(u)), Cmd+K (10.2 (x)), whose hooks Cursor documents with Agent Chat's ([CD] hooks.md:28, 695), and the interactive `agent` CLI (10.2 (v)). Local agents in the Agents Window are not offered until 10.2 (y) has run them: Cursor's page on that window says nothing about hooks ([CD] agent__agents-window.md);
    - Gemini CLI: the terminal, headless `gemini -p`, and ACP mode in Zed and JetBrains, through an extension folder (H-4); preflight 10.5.
    An adapter whose preflight has not passed is left out of the release: `--ai-hooks on` does not offer it, and the README lists it as not supported yet. Surfaces without a usable hook get the manual paths that work today: `awake -- COMMAND`, `awake -w PID` or `While` in the picker, or a timed session. Cloud sessions of every agent need nothing from the Mac. **The owner's answer.**
  - (b) Claude Code and Cursor's agent first, the owner's own setup, then Codex and Gemini CLI in a later minor version. Less to test in 2.5.0, but the owner asked for all four; under (a) the same happens by itself if their preflight is not run in time.
  - (c) (a), plus GitHub Copilot, whose one file `~/.copilot/hooks/awake.json` covers Copilot CLI and VS Code's Copilot agent (github/docs@8f330f1 content/copilot/reference/hooks-reference.md:36-40; vscode-docs@279a4a7 docs/agent-customization/hooks.md:130-141), and Google Antigravity as an experiment. More reach, but Copilot's VS Code payloads differ from its CLI's (vscode-docs hooks.md:186), and Antigravity's sources disagree about its events (2.5); neither has a preflight step, so the rule leaves them out of release 1.

### Setup

- **H-1. How do Awake's hooks get into each agent? (section 8)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too."
  - (a) `awake --ai-hooks on` writes Awake's handlers into each agent's own user-level hook file, the way that agent documents it, one handler at a time, and leaves every other handler alone:
    - Claude Code: `~/.claude/settings.json`, and each `--config-dir` folder: 13 handlers;
    - Codex: `~/.codex/hooks.json`: 10 handlers, in groups of their own at the end of each event (H-3);
    - Cursor: `~/.cursor/hooks.json`: 7 entries;
    - Gemini CLI: an extension that Awake keeps in its own folder and links into Gemini CLI: 6 handlers (H-4).
    Each handler is one shell-form command, `'<awake path>' --agent-hook AGENT VERB >/dev/null 2>&1; exit 0`, which every agent runs through a shell (K2). It refuses a file it cannot parse or that changes while it works. It writes through a symlink and keeps the file's mode. `off` removes only Awake's handlers, and also ends an automatic session that only those agents kept. The installer runs `refresh`, which updates the handlers and the path only where they are already on, and the uninstaller runs `off` for every agent and folder Awake wrote to. `print` prints each block for pasting by hand. **Recommended.**
  - (b) A plugin or extension for each agent where one exists: a Claude Code plugin with Awake's repository as its own marketplace (`/plugin marketplace add anttikaenmaki/awake`, discover-plugins.md:180-213; hooks in `hooks/hooks.json`, plugins/components.md:745-779), a Codex plugin, a Cursor plugin (Cursor installs hooks "through plugins from Customize" too, [CD] hooks.md:9), a Gemini extension. Awake would edit fewer files, but:
    - a plugin's version is not tied to the installed `awake`, and its script must find `awake`;
    - installing needs each agent's own command or a step inside a session, and the app's checkbox would have to drive them;
    - a Codex plugin still needs the trust step, plus a marketplace step (codex-rs/hooks/src/engine/discovery.rs:244-295).
  - (c) Only `print`: the user pastes each block themselves. There is no risk to their files, but there is no menu switch either, and every update that changes a hook set needs a manual edit in up to four files.

- **H-2. Lid mode of automatic sessions, and password-free mode. (5.3, W12, K8)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." One choice for all agents.
  - (a) Lid-closed by default. That needs password-free mode, because a hook has no terminal and must never pop a password dialog in the middle of a turn (hooks.md:720; Codex also starts each hook in a new session, codex-rs/hooks/src/engine/command_runner.rs:412-413).
    - The first `--ai-hooks on`, and any `on --backend awake`, refuses while password-free mode is off, and names `awake --passwordless on`.
    - The app's checkbox offers to turn password-free mode on first, which asks for the password once, as turning it on always does.
    - `--ai-hooks on --backend caffeinate` chooses lid-open sessions instead. The choice is stored, and `on` without `--backend` and `refresh` keep it.
    - If password-free mode is turned off later, automatic sessions fall back to lid-open, and `--status` says why. A lid-closed automatic session that runs at that moment can no longer be renewed, as a renewal never asks for a password: with the lid open the watcher replaces it with a lid-open one in the same pass, and with the lid closed its lease runs out within 2 minutes and the Mac sleeps (W29). The floor of a lid-closed session of the user's stops the same way (W30).
    **Recommended.**
  - (b) Lid-closed when password-free mode works, otherwise lid-open, with no refusal at setup. Less friction, but a user who never turned password-free mode on closes the lid and finds that the Mac slept.
  - (c) Lid-open only. No password-free mode is needed. But some agents already keep the Mac from idle sleep while they work: Claude Code with its own `caffeinate -i` (section 1), and reportedly Cursor with a wakelock (a forum snippet only, section 1). For them lid-open sessions add little: only the waits and the minute between turns. Codex holds none by default and Gemini CLI none at all (section 1), so for them lid-open sessions are worth more.

- **H-3. Codex's trust step. (K.3.2)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." By Codex 0.130.0 (not at 0.125.0; the release tags in between were not checked, 2.3) a hook from the user's files runs only after the user has trusted it. The trust is a hash per handler, stored in `~/.codex/config.toml` under a key made of the file, the event's snake_case label (`user_prompt_submit`) and the handler's group and position, and untrusted or changed hooks are skipped without an error (codex-rs/hooks/src/engine/discovery.rs:666-736, 796-823; codex-rs/hooks/src/lib.rs:95-123). At startup Codex's terminal UI shows "Hooks need review", with "Trust all and continue", which asks for an explicit confirmation (the title at @4b5c111 codex-rs/tui/src/startup_hooks_review.rs:233, the three choices at 263-271), and `/hooks` reviews them too. `codex exec` has no review of its own: it runs hooks already trusted in the terminal UI and skips the others silently; its only alternative is `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust`, which waives the review for every hook of that run (openai/codex@4b5c111 codex-rs/exec/src/cli.rs:168; codex-rs/hooks/src/engine/discovery.rs:715-736), and which Awake never suggests. The terminal UI trusts hooks through the app-server's `config/batchWrite`, after `hooks/list` reports each hook's `trustStatus` (@4b5c111 codex-rs/tui/src/hooks_rpc.rs:51-90; codex-rs/app-server-protocol/src/protocol/v2/plugin.rs:52-62, 558-603), so Codex's IDE extension and app could offer the same review; whether they do cannot be read, as both are closed source (10.4).
  - (a) Awake writes its handlers and never trusts them for the user. `on` ends with "Start codex in a terminal once and choose Trust all and continue, then confirm (or run /hooks); in the Codex app or IDE extension, review hooks there if it offers it. Until then Codex skips Awake's hooks." `status` reads `installed, not trusted yet` while the trust entries are missing, and `installed, review again in Codex` after a `refresh` that changed a handler's text (K13). Awake's handlers stay byte for byte the same across upgrades unless the awake path changes, and its groups never move, so the trust survives `refresh`. **Recommended**: the review is Codex's security control, and Adrafinil makes the same choice (adrafinil@6e4eece AdrafinilShared/Sources/AdrafinilShared/Installer/Integrations/CodexIntegration.swift:28-32).
  - (b) Awake writes the trust entries itself. No step for the user, but it bypasses Codex's review, and it needs a TOML writer and Codex's canonical hash (discovery.rs:768-794; codex-rs/config/src/fingerprint.rs:54-66).
  - (c) The system layer, `/etc/codex/hooks.json`, which Codex counts as managed and runs without review (discovery.rs:715-720, 825-833). Machine-wide, and it needs an administrator. Not recommended.

- **H-4. Gemini CLI: an extension, or the settings file? (K.3.4)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too."
  - (a) An extension, with `gemini-extension.json` and `hooks/hooks.json` (gemini-cli@fb972b2 docs/extensions/reference.md:240-244), kept in a folder Awake owns, `~/Library/Application Support/Awake/gemini-extension/`, and registered as a linked extension: `on` runs `gemini extensions link <folder> --consent` when it finds a `gemini` executable (packages/cli/src/commands/extensions/link.ts:64-78), and says that the user's `on` is the consent Gemini would ask for ("This extension contains Hooks which can automatically execute commands", packages/cli/src/config/extensions/consent.ts:322); otherwise it writes only `${GEMINI_CLI_HOME:-$HOME}/.gemini/extensions/awake/.gemini-extension-install.json` with `{"source": "<awake folder>", "type": "link"}`, the record `link` writes (extension-manager.ts:438-443), from which Gemini loads the extension's files (extension-manager.ts:762-764). Never a bare folder with the files under `~/.gemini/extensions`: while `security.allowedExtensions` is set, in any settings file, an extension without that record aborts Gemini CLI's startup ("An unexpected critical error occurred"; extension-manager.ts:725-731, 612-672; packages/cli/src/config/config.ts:675; packages/cli/index.ts:137-168), whereas a linked one that the allowlist does not match is only skipped with a warning (extension-manager.ts:746-750). Never `type: "local"` with Awake's folder as its own source, as an update would first delete it (extension-manager.ts:568-576). Gemini loads every folder there (extension-manager.ts:628-639) and aborts on two that declare the same name (:645-651), so `on` refuses when another folder already declares `awake`. Awake never edits the user's `settings.json`, which may hold comments that Gemini accepts (packages/cli/src/config/settings.ts:786-787) and JavaScript's `JSON.parse` refuses. Removal deletes the link folder and Awake's folder. Extension hooks also run in folders Gemini does not trust (packages/core/src/hooks/hookRegistry.ts:188-198; hookRunner.ts:64-79), where hooks from the settings files do not (hookRegistry.ts:172-186). **Recommended.**
  - (b) Merge into `~/.gemini/settings.json`, like the other agents. One mechanism everywhere, but files with comments are refused, and the hooks are skipped in every folder not yet trusted, the default since v0.24.0 (docs/changelogs/index.md:572-574).
  - (c) Only `print` for Gemini.

- **H-5. Cursor's agent also runs Claude Code's hooks. (K.3.3, W2)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." Cursor's own agent and its CLI run the hooks in `~/.claude/settings.json` too, while "Include Third-Party Plugins, Skills, and Other Configs" is on, which it is by default (mehmetbaykar/cursor-docs-skill@116359f skills/cursor-docs/references/reference__third-party-hooks.md:17, 21-41; cli__changelog.md:421). What such a hook receives on stdin Cursor does not document: the page documents the event mapping, the tool-name mapping and the output formats (reference__third-party-hooks.md:80-156, 233-247), not the input. That it is Cursor's native payload, with `conversation_id` first, is an assumption that 10.2 checks.
  - (a) Native Cursor handlers in `~/.cursor/hooks.json`, and Awake's Claude Code handlers do nothing for a Cursor run, told by either of two signs: the part of the payload before the first content key has the key `"conversation_id"`, which Cursor sends with every agent event, first in the captured payloads ([CD] hooks.md:814-846; [RO] cursor-logs.txt:12, 62), and Claude Code never sends (0 matches in hooks.md); or the hook's environment has no `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID`, which Claude Code sets in every hook command (env-vars.md:371), but has `CURSOR_VERSION`, which Cursor sets in every hook ([CD] hooks.md:1457-1460). The second sign covers a translated, Claude-shaped payload, should Cursor send one. Each Cursor turn then counts once, under Cursor, whatever the user's Cursor setting. The price: while the import is on, Cursor also runs Awake's Claude Code `PreToolUse` handler as its own `preToolUse`, a permission hook that Cursor always waits for ([CD] hooks.md:198, 701-712: no `async` option), so each Cursor tool call waits for two short Awake processes, and a rollback for Cursor must cover both files (K.9). **Recommended.**
  - (b) No Cursor handlers: let Cursor run Awake's Claude Code handlers. One file fewer, but it depends on a Cursor setting, gets no `afterAgentThought`, the first sign of a follow-up turn ([FL] docs/providers/cursor-agent.md:57, 2.4), no `postToolUseFailure` (not in Cursor's mapping, third-party-hooks.md:205-220), and every Cursor turn would be named Claude's in the status.
  - (c) Both, without the stand-down. Every Cursor turn would arrive twice, half of it under Claude's keys. Not recommended.

- **H-6. An agent installed after the feature was turned on. (K.3, M3)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too."
  - (a) `on` covers the agents found at that moment. An agent found later is not turned on by itself: Settings lists it as off, with its own checkbox, and `status` reads `off` for it while others are on. The installer's `refresh` never adds an agent. **Recommended**: Awake edits another tool's files only when the user asks.
  - (b) The app turns on every newly found agent while the feature is on. Nothing to remember, but Awake writes into a new tool's settings without being asked, and for Codex the trust step still waits for the user.
  - (c) `refresh` adds them at each Awake update. The same objection, at a time the user does not expect.

### Behaviour

- **B-1. Waiting for the user: a permission prompt, a question, a plan to approve. (5.6)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a), as part of the timings. No hook in Claude Code, Codex or Gemini CLI fires when a prompt is answered (section 2), and Cursor has no waiting signal at all (2.4). So after a waiting event the holder stays `waiting` until the approved tool has run and its post-tool event arrives. One exception from the reviews: Codex runs `PermissionRequest` before its automatic reviewer too, so with `approvals_reviewer = "auto_review"` it fires although nobody is asked (codex-rs/core/src/tools/approvals.rs:505-525; codex-rs/protocol/src/config_types.rs:178-189), and its payload does not say which (codex-rs/hooks/src/schema.rs:301-318). Codex's `PermissionRequest` is therefore a heartbeat (`tool-start`), and an approval nobody answers there costs the 15-minute idle limit, as Cursor's approval cards do; only Codex's question tool counts as waiting (5.2).
  - (a) Stay awake for 10 minutes of waiting, then, for Claude Code, take the last look (B-7): if Claude Code reports that session `busy` (the prompt was answered and the tool runs), the holder goes back to working, and otherwise the Mac may sleep. For agents without a last look the 10 minutes are the end (B-13). The 10 minutes let an answer from a phone over Remote Control reach a Mac whose lid is closed. Adrafinil's default is the same (adrafinil@6e4eece AdrafinilShared/…/Models/AdrafinilSettings.swift:3-15, 52-57). **Recommended, and the owner's answer.**
  - (b) 10 minutes and nothing more, also for Claude Code. A tool approved at minute 5 that runs 8 minutes is cut off at minute 10, and a closed Mac sleeps in the middle of it.
  - (c) Stay awake while waiting, as while working, up to the 15-minute idle limit. Simpler (no `waiting` hooks), but a forgotten prompt keeps a closed Mac awake for 15 minutes.
  - (d) Sleep after the end grace (60 s). The Mac sleeps soonest, but a phone answer after a minute finds it asleep.

- **B-2. Timings. (5.5, 5.6, 5.10)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a), with the session limit at 365 days. All are constants in `bin/awake`, with shorter values in the dry run:
  - the end grace after the last holder ends: 60 s;
  - the idle limit without any sign of work: 15 min;
  - the waiting allowance: B-1; the background allowance: B-8;
  - each automatic session's outer limit: 365 days, the maximum of every session (`MAX_DURATION_SECONDS`, 121-122). The first version had 12 hours here. Since the third revision the session itself is a 2-minute lease that the watcher renews (B-12), so the watcher enforces the 365 days from the session's start; the helper's own bound counts each end from now (awake-helper:57-59, 1115-1132).
  - (a) As listed, with no setting in the app. **Recommended, and the owner's answer.** The idle limit is longer than a foreground Bash call of Claude Code (10 minutes at most by default, tools-reference.md:160-161), and it counts from the start of the running tool, as each agent's pre-tool event sends a heartbeat (5.6). MCP tools of Claude Code may run for about 28 hours (`MCP_TOOL_TIMEOUT`, env-vars.md:483), and the user can raise `BASH_MAX_TIMEOUT_MS`: with the last look such a tool reads `busy` and goes on, up to the quiet limit (B-12), and without it the README lists it as not covered.
  - (b) A 5-minute idle limit. The Mac sleeps sooner after an interrupt that no hook reports. Safe only with the last look: without it, a 6-minute build would end the session while it runs.
  - The other options on the limit (none, or a rolling root-enforced limit) are now part of B-12.

- **B-3. Which guardrails do automatic sessions use? (5.10)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too."
  - (a) The app's Guardrails settings: `Stop when too hot`, `Stop at low battery` with its level, and `Stop when unplugged`. The CLI reads them from the app's defaults domain, as it already reads the picker's settings (1156-1191). When they are not set, the CLI's defaults apply (5%, on, off). **Recommended.**
  - (b) Always the CLI's defaults: 5%, thermal guard on, unplug guard off. No new reads, but the app's settings do not apply to automatic sessions.
  - (c) As (a), but with the unplug guard on by default for automatic sessions. A Mac carried off while an agent works then sleeps within 5 to 10 seconds of unplugging (README.md:61). It surprises users who work on battery, though: a session started on battery is affected only after the Mac has been plugged in (README.md:61).

- **B-4. A session the user started by hand. (5.9)** Answered on 2026-10-06 in two rounds. First: (a), "what the user decides should always override the automatics", now the principle at the top. Then, in the second round: "If the user has started an awake session for 10 minutes and then puts an AI agent to work which takes 2 hours, then does the session stop after 10 minutes or after 2 hours? For me stopping the session in this case after 2 hours sounds logical." That is (d), and B-19 asks one more case of it. In every option a start by hand during an automatic session (a time option, `-w`, `--`, the picker) replaces the automatic session instead of adding time to it or being refused (the handover, 5.9), and an automatic session starts only when no session runs.
  - (a) Automatic sessions never change the user's session. When it ends while an agent still works:
    - with the lid open, an automatic session follows within a pass (2 s);
    - with the lid closed, the Mac still sleeps at your session's end, even while the agent works (awake-helper:850-855), and the agent's turn stalls until you open the lid. Then an automatic session follows (B-11).
    The first round's answer, replaced by (d) for sessions with an end time; it still describes sessions tied to a process.
  - (b) While an agent works, the user's timed session is extended past its end, with a new kind of end ("until the agents stop"). The Mac stays awake with the lid closed, but it needs a new end in the helper.
  - (c) While a holder works and the lid is closed, the watcher adds 5 minutes at a time to the user's timed session. The turn does not stall, but a session can run up to 5 minutes past the work, and a lid-open session gets nothing.
  - (d) The floor: while the feature is on, a session the user started by time (a length or an end time) is a minimum. AI work keeps it going past its end. (b)'s behaviour, built with (c)'s mechanism and no new kind of end: within the last 90 s of the session, and past its end, while a holder needs it, the watcher moves its end to 2 minutes from now about every 30 s, with the helper's `extend @EPOCH` for a lid-closed session and the Caffeine state file for a lid-open one, the same renewal as the lease (W29, W30). `extend @EPOCH` only ever moves an end later (awake-helper:1128-1141), so the user's own end is never shortened. When no holder needs it any more, the renewals stop and the session ends at its last end: its own end if no renewal ran, which is the case when the work ended more than 90 s before it, and otherwise 2 minutes after the last renewal, 90 to 120 s after the work's end (B-17), as `timeout`, which sleeps a closed Mac. It is a continuation of the same session, not a new start, so B-11's rule against starts with the lid closed does not stop it. Per kind of session (5.9 has the details):
    - a length or an end time: floored, lid-open in every password mode and without root (6594-6667), lid-closed only while password-free mode works, as the renewal must never ask for a password;
    - `--indefinite`: nothing to do;
    - `-w PID` and `-- COMMAND`, with or without a time limit: never changed. Their end is a process, which the helper, its guard and the Caffeine runner apply whatever the end time (awake-helper:1393-1399, 1503-1505; 6029-6042), and `extend` cannot touch it; a time given with them is an upper bound ("at most", 3042-3045); and the user tied the session to that process on purpose (for `awake -- claude -p`, the process is the agent itself). Taking over such a session at its end, with a stop and an automatic start, would break B-11 with the lid closed and could leave a closed Mac awake with no session when the start fails (awake-helper:621-624). With the lid open, an automatic session follows within a pass; with the lid closed, the Mac sleeps when the process exits, and the README says so.
    The user's explicit actions win: a stop ends the session at once and pauses the automatics (B-5); before its own end, added time adds to its current end as today, which in the session's last 90 s the floor may already have moved up to 2 minutes from now (past its own end, a start by hand goes through the handover, B-16); turning the feature (or the agent) off stops the renewals, so the session ends at its last end: its own end, or, when the floor had already renewed it, at most 2 minutes later. **Recommended, and the owner's answer.**

- **B-5. What happens when the user stops the automatic session while the AI still works? (5.9)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." The owner, 2026-10-06: "What if the user stops the automatic session? Does it start again automatically (given Claude is still working)? This sounds logical, but is it good in practise?" A stop is a click on the icon, `Stop session`, `awake --stop` or plain `awake`, of an automatic session or of the user's own while an agent works. Stop or Esc inside an agent's window is not a stop of Awake: in the Claude Code extension it ends only the current turn, and background agents keep running (vs-code.md:172).

  The short answer: not at once, and not in the same run. Starting again while the work goes on is logical on paper, but in practice it makes the stop look broken and undoes the reasons people stop. The cases:

  | Case | (a) Pause until your next prompt | (b) Start again at once | (c) Off until turned on again | (d) Pause for a fixed time (1 h) |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | You stop, close the lid and leave, while Claude still works | The Mac sleeps; nothing starts again: no key or click came after your click on the icon, and the lid is closed; the turn stalls until you are back and type | The next tool call, seconds later, starts a session again. If it comes before the lid closes, the Mac stays awake in the bag (B-11 stops only starts with the lid already closed) | The Mac sleeps | The Mac sleeps; after the hour nothing starts with the lid closed |
  | You stop to save battery and keep working with the lid open | Off until your next prompt to an agent; a scheduled or background turn that happens to start while you type elsewhere also lifts it (the next row); while you use the Mac with the lid open, a session changes little, and the battery guard applies | The stop does nothing you can see | Off for good | Back after an hour, while you may still be on battery |
  | You stop, keep working in other apps, and a `/loop` iteration or a background report starts while you type | The pause lifts: the hook cannot tell this turn from a typed one, and you used the Mac a moment ago. A session runs while the work goes on, and the menu shows it. If you then close the lid and leave, the Mac stays awake until the work ends, as after any prompt | The same, and sooner | Stays off | Starts again after the hour |
  | You stop by mistake | `Resume now` in the menu, or your next prompt | Undone by itself | You have to find the setting | Back after an hour |
  | Claude in the Cursor panel and Codex in a terminal at once | One stop pauses both; your next prompt to either lifts it | The other agent's next tool call starts a session again | Both off | Both back after an hour |
  | You stop at night with the lid open; a `/loop` or scheduled task runs overnight | Stays off: nobody touches the Mac, so those turns lift nothing | The next iteration starts a session, and the Mac stays awake all night | Stays off | Starts again an hour later, for the rest of the night |
  | A phone prompt over Remote Control after you stopped and closed the lid | Nothing starts (lid closed, and no input at the Mac) | Nothing starts (B-11) | Nothing starts | Nothing starts |
  | You restart the Mac during a pause | The pause stands until your next prompt (W10) | No pause to keep | Stays off | Back after the hour |

  - (a) The stop ends the session and pauses automatic sessions for every agent until the user starts a new prompt themselves, at the Mac, after the stop. The pause is written by the stop itself (5.9). It is not lifted by the turn that was still running, nor by a turn that starts while nobody uses the Mac (a scheduled task, a `/loop` iteration, a background subagent's report, a message from another session, a usage-limit resume, a Codex `/goal` continuation, a Cursor follow-up, `/goal` continuation, `/loop` iteration or background-task wakeup), nor by anything while the lid is closed and closing it sleeps the Mac (B-11), nor by a start by hand, also one an agent runs. While it stands, the menu shows `Paused until your next prompt` and `Resume now`, `--status` adds `AI sessions are paused until your next prompt.`, and Settings shows the same. `Resume now`, or turning the feature off and on, also clears it. It survives a restart (W10). **Recommended.** Why:
    - The stop is the user's clearest signal; the next moment they act again is their next prompt, which is also when they would expect the feature back.
    - Stopping and leaving is safe: after a stop, a session starts again only if somebody uses the Mac and an agent's turn starts within seconds of that, so "stop, close the lid and leave" never restarts one. The one case it lets through is the third row: a turn that is not typed but starts while the user types elsewhere, which the menu then shows.
    - One pause for all agents, because there is one session for all of them: a pause per agent would let another agent start it again at once.
    - A stop by mistake costs one click (`Resume now`) or nothing (the next prompt).
    - Adrafinil, the closest tool, separates the same two things: "force release" lets the next agent hook hold again, and a "Let it sleep" pause stays until the user resumes, also across a restart (adrafinil@6e4eece AdrafinilShared/Sources/AdrafinilShared/IPC/DaemonProtocol.swift:20, 28; Models/PersistedDaemonState.swift:3-6).

    How "a prompt the user typed" is told apart differs per agent (5.9, 2.2-2.5):
    - Claude Code runs `UserPromptSubmit` also for a scheduled task or `/loop` iteration, a background subagent's report and a message from another session (hooks.md:1325-1329), in the extension and the Desktop app also for turns it starts on its own (agent-sdk/hooks.md:153), and for the continuation after a usage limit (interactive-mode.md:688). Its payload has no field that tells them apart (hooks.md:728-744, 1337-1351). A typed prompt runs it at once, as it blocks the prompt until the hook returns (hooks.md:1331).
    - Codex runs `UserPromptSubmit` for each user input, also input typed while a turn runs, but not for a `/goal` continuation or a message between agents (codex-rs/core/src/hook_runtime.rs:677-714; codex-rs/ext/goal/src/runtime.rs:474-485). It runs after pre-turn compaction and MCP startup, so it can come many seconds after Enter (codex-rs/core/src/session/turn.rs:184-379). Its app's automations probably run it too (not verified).
    - Cursor runs `beforeSubmitPrompt` "right after user hits send" ([CD] hooks.md:1243-1245), not for a follow-up turn ([FL] cursor-agent.md:57). Cursor also has `/goal` continuations, `/loop` iterations and background tasks that wake the agent ([CD] agent__overview.md:132-142; skills.md:51; release-notes__agents-window.md:190, 296); which hooks they fire: not found (section 10.2 (u)).
    - Gemini CLI runs `BeforeAgent` at the start of a prompt's turn, before any compression (packages/core/src/core/client.ts:924-946, 165-193), and again only for a continuation that another hook forces (client.ts:1031-1047).
    So the pause lifts on a `turn-start` of a main conversation (no subagent id) when all of these hold, tested in `main` (W26):
    - the lid is open, or the Mac is in closed-display mode (`lid_closed_causes_sleep` is false, B-11);
    - the Mac's last keyboard or mouse input came at least 2 s after the stop, so the click that made the stop does not count: the time now, less the input idle time, is at least the pause's `since` plus 2;
    - the input idle time is at most 5 s, or 60 s for Codex, whose hook can lag Enter. A typed prompt runs the hook within a second of Enter in Claude Code, Cursor and Gemini CLI (above); section 10 measures it and may narrow or widen the 5 s;
    - the idle time could be read: if `ioreg` fails or times out, the pause stays, and `Resume now` remains.
    macOS reports that idle time as `HIDIdleTime` in `ioreg -c IOHIDSystem` (a design assumption, measured in section 10). A phone prompt with nobody at the Mac does not lift it, which matches "typed at the Mac". A prompt queued in the Claude Code panel while a turn runs may run its hook only when it is taken up, more than 5 s after the typing (not found, measured in 10.2); it then does not lift the pause, and the README says to use `Resume now`.
  - (b) No pause: the next tool call starts a session again. See the table: a stop seems not to work, and "stop and close the lid" can leave the Mac awake in a bag.
  - (c) The pause stays until the user turns the feature off and on, or chooses `Resume now`. Safe, but it turns "stop now" into a setting change the user did not make, and the next day's work runs without the feature until they remember.
  - (d) A pause for a fixed time, such as an hour. The timer is invisible, and the session comes back while the user may be away with the lid open.
  - (e) The first version's rule: the pause lifts on the next turn of any kind that starts while the lid is open. No input check, but an overnight `/loop` or a background report starts again the session the user stopped.

- **B-6. Sounds and notifications. (5.12, M6, M8)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." The owner asked on 2026-10-06: "should the automatic AI sessions use the user's sound and notification settings, or should it have its own, or some other way?"
  - (a) Silent by default, whatever the user's sound and notification settings for their own sessions, and loud only when the user is needed. **Recommended.**
    - No start or stop sound and no `Awake started` or `Awake finished` for an automatic session. With the feature on, sessions start and end with every turn, often many times an hour, so the user's own settings would turn into constant noise; the agents already say when they finish.
    - Always told, by the app, following the user's notification permission and the app's `Sound on`: a guardrail that ends an automatic session while an agent may still be working ("Claude Code may still be working."), and a problem that stops the feature from doing what the user asked, once per cause and not at every turn (for example lid-closed sessions falling back to lid-open because password-free mode is off, or Codex asking for its hooks to be trusted again).
    - The state is shown rather than announced: the menu bar icon and the status sentence (5.11) say when an automatic session is on, and when a stop has paused them.
    - One opt-in switch, `Announce AI sessions` in Settings' `AI agents` group (M8), off by default. When on, automatic sessions follow the user's existing `Sound on` and notification settings, as the app's own sessions do. There is no separate set of AI-only sound and notification settings.
    - A session the user started keeps the user's settings, also when AI work keeps it past its own end (the floor, B-4): it was announced at its start by those settings, and its end is announced, if they say so, when it really ends, after the work.
  - (b) Automatic sessions always follow the user's own settings: a sound and a notification at every turn for anyone with `Sound on`.
  - (c) A separate set of AI-only sound and notification settings: more to explain, for little gain over (a)'s one switch.
  - (d) Nothing at all, guardrail ends and problems included: the user may not learn that the Mac is about to sleep while an agent works.

- **B-7. A last look before the Mac may sleep, for Claude Code. (5.6, W21)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." When a time rule would end the last Claude Code holder that keeps an automatic session (the end grace, the waiting allowance, the idle limit, the background allowance), and when a tool starts for a session whose turn had ended and whose holder was dropped, the watcher runs `claude agents --json` once. That is "the supported way to read session state from outside Claude Code" (agent-view.md:779), and it gives each live session's `pid` and `status`, `busy`, `waiting` or `idle` (agent-view.md:764-777). A main-conversation holder whose process reads `busy` goes back to working; subagent holders share their session's process (hooks.md:269), so the last look never revives them. Anything else, or a failed call, lets the time rule apply. A holder whose process a successful call does not list gets B-7 (b)'s timings from then on, and so does a holder whose hooks ran with another `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, whose sessions a supervisor of the default folder does not see (agent-view.md:817). Codex, Cursor and Gemini CLI document nothing like it (2.3-2.5): their holders follow the time rules alone (B-13).
  - (a) Yes, if section 10 shows that it works with the installed Claude Code, lists the sessions of the extension inside Cursor (the owner's setup, not documented), starts no background supervisor, and takes under 1 s; otherwise (b). The docs say that `claude agents` starts the supervisor on demand, a background service that also restarts sessions (agent-view.md:758, 802-809, 918, 932); whether the `--json` form does is checked with `claude daemon status` before and after (10.3 (f)). If it does, expect (b) to become the default, or the last look to run only while a supervisor already runs. It covers what no hook signals: a prompt answered (B-1), a `/goal` or Stop-hook continuation that starts its first tool late, a plan run that thinks before its first tool, an MCP tool longer than the idle limit. It is one call at each expiry, not polling. **Recommended.**
  - (b) No. Time rules only, with the waiting allowance at 20 minutes (B-1) and the end grace at 3 minutes, so that a slow Stop hook or a plan run is covered more often. Each normal reply then keeps a closed Mac awake 3 minutes instead of 1.
  - (c) As (a), and also poll it during gaps of 2 minutes or more without hooks. The Mac sleeps sooner after an interrupt, which matters most in the extension, where no `idle_prompt` fires (2.2). But each poll starts `claude`, on a feature in research preview (agent-view.md:22).

- **B-8. Background work and scheduled wakeups after a turn, for Claude Code. (5.6, W9)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." `Stop` reports `background_tasks` (shells, subagents, monitors, workflows) and `session_crons` (scheduled wakeups and `/loop`) (hooks.md:2583-2605). Local background commands have no time limit (tools-reference.md:186, 190), so a dev server or `tail -f` can run for days. Codex's `Stop` (codex-rs/hooks/src/schema.rs:585-601), Cursor's `stop` ([CD] hooks.md:1304-1324) and Gemini's `AfterAgent` report no background work, so for them background work after a turn does not count. Cursor also has background tasks that wake the agent later ([CD] release-notes__agents-window.md:190), unreported in `stop`.
  - (a) Background tasks keep the Mac awake for 1 hour, counted from the first `Stop` that reports some. Later `Stop`s that still report some do not renew it; only a `Stop` with none resets it. Scheduled wakeups (`session_crons`) do not count as background work: a `/loop` whose next turn starts more than 60 s after the previous one's end lets the Mac sleep between iterations, and the README suggests a timed session for such a `/loop` that should run with the lid closed. A `/loop` whose turns start within 60 s of the previous one's end keeps the session going like continuous work, as the next `turn-start` lifts the `ended` holder within the grace (5.6), up to the presence limit (B-18). A long-lived dev server then costs at most one hour, once. **Recommended, and the owner's timing.**
  - (b) 1 hour, renewed at every `Stop` that reports background work. A dev server started once then keeps a closed Mac awake for an hour after every turn.
  - (c) As (a), and a non-empty `session_crons` counts as background work, under the same allowance.
  - (d) None. Only background subagents keep the Mac awake, through their own holders (5.7). A background build ends with the Mac asleep if the lid is closed.

- **B-9. Remote Control between turns, for Claude Code. (5.6)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." Remote Control from a phone is a main reason to keep a closed Mac awake while away. A sleeping Mac drops the session until it wakes (remote-control.md:16-18). Codex can also be driven from the ChatGPT app through its daemon (codex-rs/app-server-daemon/README.md:166-177), but its hook payloads carry no marker for it, so Codex turns get the plain 60 s.
  - (a) As for any turn: the Mac may sleep 60 s after each reply, so the phone cannot send the next prompt to a closed Mac. The README points to `awake -w <claude PID>`.
  - (b) While Remote Control is connected, the end of a turn keeps the holder for the waiting allowance (10 minutes) instead of 60 s. A phone prompt within those 10 minutes reaches the Mac, and its turn starts the next 10 minutes. Hooks see `CLAUDE_CODE_BRIDGE_SESSION_ID` exactly while the connection is up (env-vars.md:220, since 2.1.199), so this costs one builtin test in the hook. With "Enable Remote Control for all sessions" (remote-control.md:180-193) every turn gets 10 minutes. **Recommended.**
  - (c) Keep the Mac awake for the whole connection. No hook reports the connection's end, so the holder would need a time limit anyway.

- **B-10. Usage-limit and API-error waits. (5.6)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." A claude.ai usage limit stops Claude in the middle of a task. Claude Code then waits and goes on by itself at the reset, which is on by default for subscriptions (interactive-mode.md:657), through a prompt that runs `UserPromptSubmit` (interactive-mode.md:688). `/goal` retries by itself after an overloaded server (goal.md:145). A Codex turn that fails on an API error or a usage limit fires neither `Stop` nor `Interrupt` (codex-rs/core/src/session/turn.rs:824-840), so its holder stays working until the idle limit, whatever is chosen here.
  - (a) `StopFailure` ends the turn like `Stop`. With the lid closed the Mac sleeps a minute later. If it sleeps more than about 30 minutes across the reset, Claude Code waits for Enter (interactive-mode.md:671). The README says so. **Recommended**: the wait can last hours, and nothing signals its end before the reset.
  - (b) As (a) for `rate_limit`, but `StopFailure` with `overloaded` or `server_error` (hooks.md:2679) keeps the holder for the waiting allowance, so that `/goal`'s retries run on a closed Mac.
  - (c) Keep the Mac awake until the reset.

- **B-11. May an automatic session start while the lid is closed? (5.10, W20)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too."
  - (a) No, unless the Mac is in closed-display mode. An automatic session starts only while the lid is open, or closed on an external display (macOS's closed-display mode), which "the lid is open" means below. Once a session runs it goes on with the lid closed. So a `/loop` iteration, a scheduled task, a Desktop catch-up run on wake (desktop-scheduled-tasks.md:74) or a background report that fires while a sleeping Mac is briefly awake never starts a session in a bag. Neither does a holder that outlived a sleep, nor the end of the user's own session with the lid closed (B-4). The test is the helper's own: the lid is closed and `AppleClamshellCausesSleep` is `Yes` (awake-helper:604-619), which `bin/awake` gets as `lid_closed_causes_sleep`; today's `lid_is_closed` tests only `AppleClamshellState` (2385-2391) and would block every docked MacBook. A docked Mac needs the feature too: closed-display mode prevents only the sleep that closing the lid causes, and idle sleep still follows the Energy settings unless "Prevent automatic sleeping on power adapter when the display is off" is on (a user forum, https://discussions.apple.com/thread/254721961; Apple's own pages are blocked here), while Codex holds no idle-sleep assertion by default and Gemini CLI none (section 1). **Recommended.**
  - (b) Yes, except after a stop by the user or after a session ended with the lid closed, until the lid has been open. A wakeup after a normal end can still start a lid-closed session in a bag.
  - (c) Yes, always.
  - (d) As (a), but closed-display mode counts as closed. Simpler to explain, but a docked MacBook never gets an automatic session, and a typed prompt there never lifts a stop pause.

- **B-12. What ends an automatic session, now that it may run 365 days? (5.10, 5.14, W29)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "Since the cap is 365 days, it is crucial that automatic sessions stop in any imaginable situation." The 12-hour limit was the one bound that root enforced whatever the watcher did. With the owner's 365 days it bounds nothing in practice, and the previous revision's bounds all lived in the watcher: a watcher that hung in a way its replacement (W27) missed, or one alive but wrong, was not bounded by root. These hold in every option, as layers:
  - the work's end: each holder ends at a turn's end (plus 60 s), after 10 minutes of waiting, 1 hour of background work, or 15 minutes without any sign of work (B-1, B-2, B-8), with the 2-hour quiet limit on the last look;
  - the watcher's PID tie: the session is tied to the watcher's PID, so the helper or the Caffeine runner ends it within 2 s when the watcher exits or is killed (awake-helper:1393-1398; 6029-6041);
  - the replacement of a stuck watcher by a later hook (W27);
  - the agent's process: a holder whose process exits is dropped at once (5.8);
  - the guardrails, which the helper checks on its own (awake-helper:1357-1400; B-3), and no start with the lid closed (B-11);
  - the presence limit, if B-18 is answered (a): no renewal once nobody has used the Mac for 24 hours;
  - the user: a stop, or `--ai-hooks off`.
  - (a) **A renewable lease that root ends.** An automatic session is an ordinary timed session with a short end: it starts with `--duration-seconds 120` (and `-w` on the watcher, as before), and while any holder needs it the watcher moves its end to 2 minutes from now whenever 90 s or less are left, about every 30 s: `extend UID @<now+120>` through the helper in password-free mode, or the Caffeine state file for a lid-open session, without root. When nothing renews it, for whatever reason (the watcher dead, hung, frozen or buggy, the log lost, the disk full, Awake updated mid-run, a clock change, a sleep, a logout), the helper's timer or the Caffeine runner ends it at that end, as `timeout`, within about a second of it (probe: 0.23 to 0.80 s lid-closed, 0.11 to 0.64 s lid-open), as it ends any timed session, and with the lid closed the helper sleeps the Mac (awake-helper:850-855). So the worst case after renewals stop is 2 minutes, whatever the watcher does. That needs one more thing, found by a review probe: nothing the watcher leaves behind may hold up the end. A renewal run in a subshell of the watcher records the watcher's PID in the main lock and in the lid-open deadline lock (`$$` in a bash 3.2 subshell is the parent's, 1452-1460, 5681), and the Caffeine runner takes that deadline lock to end a lid-open session at its end (5980-5990), waiting as long as the lock's PID lives (5671-5672); so with a renewal stuck inside the lock, a lid-open session whose end was 4 s away still ran 20 s later, and still after the stuck subshell alone was killed, until the watcher itself died; a user's `--stop` meanwhile took 32.6 s (review probe `agent-plan3/review-end/probe-end2.out`). So each renewal and each explicit end runs as its own short `awake` process (`--agent-renew`, `--agent-end`) under a time limit, 8 s and 60 s; one killed at its limit leaves locks whose holder is dead, which `acquire_main_lock` and `acquire_deadline_lock` take over at once (1340-1354, 5671), and the runner ends the session at its end (W29). Three failures are not renewals stopping, as a healthy watcher keeps renewing while its holders read working: hooks that fall silent, an agent that crashes, and an agent that never stops (a loop whose turns follow each other within the 60-s grace, a `/goal` or a Stop hook that always continues, a retry loop). The holder rules end the first two (within seconds for a crash, 15 minutes or at most 2 hours for silence), and then the renewals stop; the third is real work by the holder rules, which only the presence limit (B-18), the guardrails, the user and the outer limit end. The watcher adds gates that do not depend on its holder rules: no renewal once the newest hook event of any agent is more than 2 hours old (the quiet limit), with every event time clamped to the watcher's own clock so that a clock set back cannot make old events look new (W3); none once `agent-events` is no longer the file it reads; none for a session whose end has already passed, which the helper's `extend` would otherwise bring back (it checks only that the timer lives, awake-helper:1072-1076; review probe `probe-end.out`, section 3); and, with B-18 (a), none once nobody has used the Mac for 24 hours. It all works on today's helper: a session tied to a PID with a duration (awake-helper:888-1028), `extend @EPOCH`, which only moves an end later (1034-1154), the timer's re-read of the end on every pass (1339-1348), and `finish_session`'s re-check under the helper's lock, which lets a renewal that races the timer win (808-814). In the dry run, a stand-in watcher's session ended 3.09 s after `kill -STOP` with a 4-second lease, as `timeout` with one mock `sleepnow`; 4.04 s after it stopped renewing while alive; and 1.03 s after `kill -9` through the PID tie (research probe, `agent-plan3/research-awake/probe-lease.out`). The cost: in password-free mode two helper runs a minute while a lid-closed automatic session runs, each a `sudo -n` with the helper's own battery and thermal reads (awake-helper:1104-1113), about 75 to 155 ms on a Mac (estimate, measured in 10.6), plus one parse of `bin/awake` for the renewal's own process, about 25 ms on Linux; no separate password-free check runs while renewals succeed, as transport `none` never prompts (W29); a lid-open session renews without root. W.4 and 5.14 have the rest. The 365 days stay the outer limit: the watcher renews nothing past 365 days after the session's start, as the helper bounds each end only from now (awake-helper:1115-1132). **Recommended, and the owner's answer.** Adrafinil has nothing like it: its hold time limits, its CPU sweep and its 24-hour backstop all run in its user-level daemon, and its root helper's only check of its own is a dead-man switch that clears the block 60 s after the daemon's last connection drops, so a daemon that hangs but stays connected keeps a closed Mac awake (section 1). The lease is bounded by root in exactly that case.
  - (b) The previous revision's answer: the layers above, without the lease, with the session's end at 365 days. A watcher that hangs in a way W27 misses (it beats but does nothing else, or no hook comes to replace it), or that is alive but wrong, keeps a closed Mac awake until a guardrail or the user ends the session.
  - (c) (b), plus the first version's rolling 2-hour limit renewed through the CLI's add-time path (`awake --start --until @now+7200`). Root bounds a hung watcher at 2 hours, not 2 minutes, and each renewal through the CLI costs about 3.6 times as much as one inside the watcher (probe: 227 ms against 63 ms on Linux) and could extend a session the user just started, as it carries no token (probe-lease.out, step 6).

- **B-13. Timings for the agents without a last look: Codex, Cursor and Gemini CLI. (5.6, W7)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too." For them the time rules decide alone.
  - (a) The same values as for Claude Code: 60 s, 10 minutes of waiting, the 15-minute idle limit, as the owner chose. The Mac sleeps soonest. Not covered, and named in the README:
    - a tool approved after a wait that runs past the 10-minute allowance (Codex and Gemini CLI, whose waiting events B-1 uses);
    - a continuation that starts its first tool more than 60 s after the turn's end, with the lid closed;
    - a single tool call longer than 15 minutes, which sends no event while it runs. Gemini's shell tool is killed only after 300 s without output (packages/cli/src/config/settingsSchema.ts:1646-1654), so a chatty build can run past it; for Codex and Cursor the tool limits were not found.
    And the other way, a holder that stays too long: a Codex subagent stopped by Esc sends nothing, as Codex runs `Interrupt` and `SessionEnd` for the main conversation only and `SubagentStop` only when a subagent's turn completes normally (openai/codex@4b5c111 codex-rs/core/src/hook_runtime.rs:471-509; codex-rs/core/src/session/turn.rs:661-669), so its `codex:a:` holder keeps the session up to the 15-minute idle limit.
    Cursor sends nothing while an approval card waits ([FL] cursor-agent.md:10-13), and Codex's `PermissionRequest` cannot tell the user's approval from its automatic reviewer's (B-1), so in both a wait counts as a running tool, up to the idle limit. **Recommended**: the owner's answer, and the cases are rare.
  - (b) For these agents, B-7 (b)'s fallback: waiting 20 minutes and grace 3 minutes. Fewer cut-off tools, but each reply keeps a closed Mac awake 3 minutes instead of 1.

- **B-14. Added time during an automatic session. (5.9, M4)** New from the reviews. The user clicks the app's time item, or runs `awake --duration 1h`, while an automatic session runs. Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "For B-14, it makes sense for me that added time works as proposed."
  - (a) The user's own session of 1 hour replaces the automatic one (the handover). It then works exactly as any session the user starts by time, which now includes the floor (B-4 (d)): it lasts the hour, or longer while AI still works, and ends about 2 minutes after the work if that is later. The app's item reads `Keep awake for 1 hour`, not `Add 1 hour`, and the CLI says "Replaced the automatic session: Awake now runs for 1 hour, and longer while AI works." (or, where the floor cannot apply, a lid-closed session with password-free mode off, "…for 1 hour."). **Recommended, and the owner's answer.**
  - (b) At least that, as a new kind of session end. What (a) now does through the floor, with no new kind of end.
  - (c) The app hides the item during automatic sessions, as an earlier revision did. The user loses the control, and the CLI still has to do (a) or (b).

- **B-15. The lease's length. (W29)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the recommendations for B-15 to B-19." The owner asked that automatic sessions end "in any imaginable situation"; a lease of a few minutes that only the watcher renews does that.
  - (a) 2 minutes, renewed when 90 s or less are left, so about every 30 s. The worst case after renewals stop is 2 minutes, the low end of the owner's range. It tolerates 90 s of failed renewals, each retried at the next pass, far longer than a user's command may hold the main lock (about 10 s, 1416-1463) or a renewal may take (8 s at most, W29). And the end is never less than 88 s ahead while a holder renews (90 s less a 2-s pass), more than the 60-s end grace plus a 5-s last look plus a 2-s pass (67 s): the rule `threshold − pass > grace + last look + pass`, which the watcher checks at its start with the constants in use (W7). So the last look at the end of the grace still runs while the end is ahead, and a holder it revives keeps the same session; with a lease of 60 s or less the session could time out during the last look, and with the lid closed B-11 would then forbid a new one, a false sleep in the middle of the work. A longer grace, B-7 (b)'s 3 minutes, would break the rule, so a holder in its grace renews too, but only up to its grace's end plus the last look and a pass (5.4). It costs two helper runs a minute while a lid-closed automatic session runs. **Recommended.**
  - (b) 3 minutes, renewed when 2 minutes or less are left, so about every 60 s. Half the helper runs and half of sudo's log lines, with a worst case of 3 minutes.
  - (c) 5 minutes, renewed every 2 minutes: the top of the owner's range, and a fifth of (a)'s helper runs, but a closed Mac may stay awake 5 minutes after the watcher fails.

- **B-16. A start by hand while the floor keeps the user's session past its own end. (5.9)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the recommendations for B-15 to B-19." Past its own end, the user's timed session runs only because AI works.
  - (a) From then on it counts as automatic for a start by hand: `-w`, `--`, a time option and `--if-off` replace it through the handover (5.9), with no pause, as for an automatic session. Before its own end it is the user's timed session, as today: a time option adds to it, and `-w` and `--` are refused until the user stops it. **Recommended**: past its end the user has nothing left of it to keep, and a refusal would push them to a stop, which also pauses the automatics.
  - (b) It stays the user's session to the end: a time option adds to its current end, and `-w` and `--` are refused (7262-7266) until the user runs `awake --stop`, which pauses the automatics until the next typed prompt.

- **B-17. When a floored session ends. (5.9, W30)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the recommendations for B-15 to B-19." The owner's timings stay: an automatic session ends 60 s after the work (W6). A floored session cannot be ended the same way without misdescribing it, and `extend` cannot shorten it.
  - (a) The renewals stop when no holder needs the session, and it ends at its last end, as `timeout`: the later of the user's own end and 2 minutes after the last renewal. Renewals run only from 90 s before the user's own end, so work that ends earlier leaves the session to end at its own end, and work that goes on into those 90 s or past them ends it 90 to 120 s after the last turn's end. It is announced as "Awake finished" where notifications are on, as for any timed session, and a closed Mac sleeps. No new end path, and no helper change. **Recommended.**
  - (b) Exactly 60 s after the work, through W6's `command-finished`. The end is then recorded as `process_exited`, which the CLI and the app announce as "Awake finished: the process it waited for exited" (5489; InstallSupport.swift:389-427) for a session that waited for no process, so both texts would need a branch for a session without a watched process.

- **B-18. What ends an agent that never stops? (5.10, 5.14, W31)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the recommendations for B-15 to B-19." The lease bounds every failure that stops the renewals, but a healthy watcher keeps renewing while events keep coming, as it should for real work. Some work never ends by itself: a `/loop` whose turns start within the 60-s grace of the previous one's end (B-8), a `/goal` or a Stop hook that always continues (goal.md:120; hooks.md:2571-2573), Codex's `/goal`, a Cursor follow-up loop, or an agent stuck retrying a tool every few seconds. On AC power with the lid closed, only the guardrails, the user and the 365-day outer limit end such a session. The first version's 12-hour cap bounded this case, and Adrafinil's 24-hour backstop does (section 1); the owner: "it is crucial that automatic sessions stop in any imaginable situation", and also "it could be possible to have really long automatic AI runs" (B-2).
  - (a) **A presence limit of 24 hours.** The watcher renews nothing, neither the lease nor the floor past the user's own end, once nobody has used the Mac's keyboard or mouse for 24 hours. It reads the Mac's input idle time (`HIDIdleTime`, the same `ioreg` read and parser as W26) every 30 s with W28's reads, keeps the time of the last input in `agent-state`, which a new watcher reads back, and counts a failed read as no input. Once the limit is reached, the lease runs out within 2 minutes and a closed Mac sleeps, as `timeout`; no automatic session starts until the limit lifts, which the status shows as a pause (`agent_paused: "away"`, `AI sessions are paused: nobody has used this Mac for 24 hours.`). The next keyboard or mouse input at the Mac lifts it within 30 s, and so does `Resume now` or `awake --ai-hooks resume` (an internal `agent-resume` line, W31) or turning the feature off and on. A start by hand does not lift it, but runs to its own end, so a long unattended run is a timed session (`awake --duration 3d`) or `awake -- COMMAND`. It is macOS's own idle rule with a 24-hour timer: a run of days goes on as long as the user touches the Mac once a day, and the closed Mac in a bag sleeps at the latest a day after the user left it. **Recommended**, as the one bound for this case that does not cut off a user who is at the Mac.
  - (b) The same, counted from the last prompt typed at the Mac (W26's input test, run by the watcher when it reads a `turn-start`) instead of any input. Narrower: a user working in other apps while an agent loops would be cut off after a day, and the watcher needs an `ioreg` at every prompt.
  - (c) Adrafinil's backstop: 24 hours after a holder's first start, whatever happens (IdleReleaseEvaluator.swift:27-40). Simple, but it cuts off a user who works with one conversation all day, and long automatic runs, which the owner wants possible.
  - (d) None, as in the third revision: the guardrails, the user and the 365-day outer limit. A loop on AC power with the lid closed keeps the Mac awake until one of them acts; the README says so.

- **B-19. A timed start by hand during a stop pause. (5.9)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the recommendations for B-15 to B-19." The user stops a session while Claude works, then runs `awake --duration 1h` without typing a prompt. The pause stands, so the hour is not floored: it ends after the hour even if Claude still works, and the floor returns only with the next typed prompt or `Resume now`.
  - (a) No floor during a pause, as now. A start by hand never lifts a pause (W10): otherwise an agent that runs `awake --duration 2m` through its shell tool after the user's stop would get a session that its own work keeps going for as long as it works, which undoes the stop. **Recommended.** A user who wants the floor back types the next prompt or chooses `Resume now`.
  - (b) A timed start by hand during a stop pause is floored, which lifts the pause for that session only. It follows the floor rule the owner asked for (B-4 (d): a session the user started by time is a minimum while AI works) more literally, but an agent's own timed start would escape the user's stop the same way.

### Release

- **R-1. Where does the code go while 2.4.0 is in its QA? (section 11)** Answered on 2026-10-06: (a). The owner: "I agree with the remaining recommendations too."
  - (a) A branch `dev-2.5` from `dev`, with a draft PR into `dev` so that CI runs. It is merged once 2.4.0, and any 2.4.x, has shipped, as faster-start-stop-2.md question X-2 (a) described. The CHANGELOG entries go in one commit after that merge (faster-start-stop-2.md X-4 (a)). **Recommended.**
  - (b) No code until 2.4.0 ships, then on `dev`.
  - (c) Into 2.4.0. That changes the build under QA. Not recommended.

## How this plan was checked

- **Code.** Written on Linux, from the code at `a9cd6d1`, which `1bbb06f` and `f6f79f1` do not change. Every line number in `bin/awake`, `bin/awake-helper`, the app and the scripts was read at that code, and the third revision's claims about the helper's `extend`, its timer and guard, the Caffeine runner, `run_helper`, `run_with_timeout` and the main lock were read again at `f6f79f1`, and the finishing pass read there again the deadline lock (5652-5682), the Caffeine runner's end (5940-5991) and the worker's cleanup (6144), `change_caffeinate_session_end` (6594-6667), the prompt's fingerprint (1466-1484), `run_with_timeout` (2339-2363), `helper_runs_without_password` (4052-4055), and the helper's `extend` (awake-helper:1060-1141), `finish_session` (800-856) and timer (1318-1350).
- **Sources of the first version.** Three research passes: Adrafinil and the other tools, Claude Code's hooks, and Awake's own code. Their notes and copies are in the scratch folder `agent-plan/research-*`.
- **Sources of the revision.** Five more passes on 2026-10-06, one per agent and one for the others, with their copies in the scratch folder `agent-plan2/research-*`:
  - Claude Code on every surface, the VS Code and Cursor extension included. The pages the first version cites were downloaded again and are byte for byte the same.
  - Codex, from its source code: openai/codex at `551bd409` (main on 2026-10-06; the latest release is 0.160.1 of 2026-10-05, https://github.com/openai/codex/releases). Release versions come from the files at each `rust-v*` tag.
