   - Terminal 1's turn ends with a background subagent still running. `Stop` has a background task, and the subagent's `a:` holder keeps working through its own tool calls.
   - The subagent's `SubagentStop` and the 60 s grace end the session, unless terminal 2 is still within its 10 minutes.
4. **Remote Control over an evening** (B-9 (b)). Each reply leaves the holder `remote` for 10 minutes, so a prompt from the phone within that time reaches the closed Mac and starts the next turn. After 10 minutes without one, the Mac sleeps.
5. **`/goal` with a slow check.** After each turn, `/goal`'s Stop hook asks a model whether the goal is met (goal.md:120). If not, Claude goes on, and its next tool's `PreToolUse` puts the holder back to working, even when that comes later than the grace: the last look reads `busy`. A `/goal` that runs for days keeps its session, renewed every 30 s, as its events keep coming (B-12), for as long as the user touches the Mac at least once a day; once nobody has used it for 24 hours, the presence limit stops the renewals and the session ends within 2 minutes (B-18 (a)). A run that should go on unattended for longer gets a timed session, such as `awake --duration 3d`, which lasts its 3 days whatever the presence limit says.
6. **The owner's case: 10 minutes, then 2 hours of Claude, lid closed** (B-4 (d)). Password-free mode is on. The user runs `awake --duration 10m`, gives Claude a task of about 2 hours, closes the lid and leaves. While Claude works, `--status` would read `Awake is on and has 9 minutes left, or longer while Claude works.` (counting to the session's own end). At about 8.5 minutes, 90 s before that end, the watcher starts renewing it to 2 minutes from now, every 30 s, with the same token, each renewal a short `awake --agent-renew` process; after 10 minutes `--status` would read `Awake is on while Claude works (your session's own time ended at 14:10).` Claude's last reply comes after 2 hours: `Stop` ends the holder, the renewals stop, the grace and the last look pass, and 90 to 120 s after the reply the session ends as `timeout`; the helper sleeps the Mac. Had the work ended after 5 minutes, the session would have ended at its own 10 minutes, untouched; had it ended at 9 minutes, after the first renewals, the session would have ended 90 to 120 s later, a little past its own 10 minutes. Had the watcher frozen at minute 40, the helper would have ended the session within 2 minutes of the last renewal and slept the Mac.
7. **The same with password-free mode off.** The floor cannot renew a lid-closed session without a prompt, so the session ends at 10 minutes and the helper sleeps the Mac (B-4 (a)); no automatic session starts in the bag (B-11). When the user opens the lid, the turn goes on and a lid-open automatic session starts within a pass.
8. **`awake -w <build PID>` with the lid closed while Codex works.** The session ends when the build exits, as the user tied it to the build: the helper ends it as `process_exited` and sleeps the Mac at once, although Codex still works (5.9). No automatic session starts in the bag. With the lid open, an automatic session would have followed within a pass.
9. **A watcher frozen at night.** An automatic session runs with the lid closed; at 03:00 the watcher stops (a bug, a debugger, `kill -STOP`). Nothing renews the lease, so within 2 minutes the helper's timer ends the session as `timeout` and sleeps the Mac (5.14). Were the watcher killed instead, the PID tie would end it within about 2 s. When the user opens the lid in the morning, the next hook finds the stale watcher, replaces it at the second look (W27), and work that is still going gets a new session within a pass.
10. **Esc in Codex.** `Interrupt` ends the turn like `Stop`; 60 s later the session ends. A Codex turn that fails on a usage limit sends nothing, and its holder ends at the 15-minute idle limit (B-10).
11. **Gemini CLI with a declined prompt.** `BeforeTool`, then `Notification` puts the holder in `waiting`. The user declines: no `AfterAgent` comes (2.5), so the holder ends after the 10-minute allowance. Had the user approved, `AfterTool` would have put it back to working.
12. **A stop while two agents work.** The Claude Code panel and Codex in a terminal both work; the user clicks the icon. The session ends as `stopped`, and the pause stands for both. Codex's next tool call starts nothing. An hour later the user types a prompt into the Claude Code panel: the lid is open, and the Enter key came after the stop and a moment before the hook, so the pause goes, and a session starts for both agents' holders.
13. **A docked MacBook.** The lid is closed on an external display, and Codex works in a terminal. Closed-display mode counts as open (B-11), so `UserPromptSubmit` starts a session, which keeps the Mac from idle sleep while the display sleeps. The user unplugs the display and leaves: the session runs on until the work ends, as after any prompt, and then the Mac sleeps.
14. **Turning on `Stop when unplugged` before leaving.** An automatic session runs, started without the unplug guard. Within 30 s the watcher replaces it with one that has the guard (W28); unplugging then ends it within 5 to 10 seconds (README.md:61), and the guardrail pause holds automatic sessions back until the Mac is on power again.
15. **An agent stuck in a loop overnight.** The user gives Claude a task, closes the lid at 18:00 and leaves; the task ends in a Stop hook that always asks for another turn, so turns follow each other all night, each within the grace. Every holder rule reads it as work, so the renewals go on. At 18:00 the next day, 24 hours after the user last touched the Mac, the presence limit stops them (B-18 (a)); within 2 minutes the helper ends the session as `timeout` and sleeps the Mac. The menu then shows `Paused: nobody used this Mac for 24 hours`; the user's next keystroke at the Mac lifts it. Without B-18 (a), only the guardrails, the user or the 365-day limit would end it.
16. **`awake --duration 1h` over SSH, lid closed, while Codex works.** The handover checks the start's refusals first: the battery is above the minimum, so it stops the automatic session and starts the user's hour, which the floor then keeps while Codex works. Had the helper failed the start, `main` would have started a 1-second session that ends as `timeout`, so the closed Mac sleeps instead of staying awake with no session (5.9).
17. **The clock set back during work.** A VM restore sets the clock back 3 hours while a Claude Code holder is working. The watcher's next pass sees `now` earlier than the last pass, clamps every stored time to `now`, and logs it; the holder then has one fresh idle limit, and if no event comes, it ends 15 minutes later, instead of after 3 hours and 15 minutes (5.6).

### 5.14 What ends a session when something fails

The owner: "it is crucial that automatic sessions stop in any imaginable situation." With the lease (B-12 (a), W29), the guarantee is the root helper's own timer, or for a lid-open session the Caffeine runner: whatever stops the renewals, the session ends at its end, at most 2 minutes after the last renewal, as `timeout`, which sleeps a closed Mac (awake-helper:1320-1348, 850-855; 5940-5991). For that guarantee to hold, nothing may hold up the timer or the runner at the end: each renewal and each explicit end runs as its own `awake` process under a time limit (8 s and 60 s), so one that hangs is killed and its locks, which name a dead process, are taken over at once (W29). The table assumes the lease of 2 minutes renewed when 90 s or less are left (B-15 (a)), the watcher's pass of 2 s, and the presence limit of B-18 (a). "Worst case" counts from the failure. Rows 1 to 41 are about automatic sessions; rows 42 to 45 about a session of the user's that the floor keeps.

| # | What fails | What ends the session | Worst case |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The watcher is killed, crashes, exits with a session running, or dies at logout | The PID tie: the helper's timer (`kill -0` every 2 s, the start time every 10 s, awake-helper:1393-1399, 537-549) or the Caffeine runner (6029-6042), as `process_exited`, which sleeps a closed Mac. The lease also ends it, if the tie should not | About 2 to 3 s (probe: 1.03 s); 2 minutes at most |
| 2 | The watcher hangs: a loop, a call without a time limit, a deadlock on `agent-lock/` or the main lock | The lease, through the helper's timer or the runner, as `timeout`. A later hook also replaces the watcher at its second look (W27), which only gets a working watcher back | 2 minutes after the last renewal, plus about 1 s (probe: 3.09 s after `kill -STOP` with a 4-second lease) |
| 3 | The watcher is frozen (`kill -STOP`, a debugger) | The lease, as in row 2. After `kill -CONT` the watcher sees an end it did not cause: no pause, and with the lid closed B-11 | As row 2 |
| 4 | The watcher is alive but wrong: a bug in the holder rules keeps a holder that should have ended | The freshness gate: no renewal once the newest hook event of any agent is more than 2 hours old, by event times clamped to the watcher's clock (5.6, W29); then the lease. The guardrails and the user's stop at any time | 2 hours after the last hook event, plus 2 minutes |
| 5 | The watcher is alive and wrong in its holder rules and its freshness gate alike, while hooks keep arriving | The presence limit, if the watcher still applies it (24 hours after the last input at the Mac, B-18 (a)); the guardrails, the user, and the watcher's 365-day outer limit. Root does not bound this case, as every one of these gates lives in the watcher; it is the power that any program running as the user already has with `awake --indefinite` in password-free mode (README.md:103; W.4) | Not bounded by root |
| 6 | The watcher is replaced at an upgrade, with the lid open (5.8) | TERM, then the PID tie; the new watcher starts a new session within a pass | About 3 s |
| 7 | The watcher's PID is reused by another process after its death | The helper's and the runner's start-time check (awake-helper:537-549; 6029-6042) | About 12 s; 2 minutes at most |
| 8 | A renewal is refused at the battery guardrail (exit 4) | The timer's battery check ends the session as `low_battery` (every 60 s, every 20 s at 15% or less, awake-helper:1357-1373); otherwise the lease. The guardrail pause follows (5.10) | 60 s (probe: 1 s with the dry run's checks every second) |
| 9 | A renewal is refused at critical heat (exit 8) | The timer's thermal check ends it as `overheated` (every 30 s); the guardrail pause follows | 30 s (probe: about 1 s) |
| 10 | The helper is busy (exit 1 after its 10-s lock wait) | The next pass tries again; a session ends early only after 90 s of failed renewals, through the lease | 2 minutes |
| 11 | `sudo` is refused: password-free mode turned off, the sudoers file changed, the helper moved | Transport `none`, so no prompt (W12); the lease ends the session as `timeout`. With the lid open the watcher replaces it with a lid-open session in the same pass (W28's fallback) | 2 minutes |
| 12 | The helper exits 6 (a session of another helper version) or 64, or is not ready, so that `run_helper` would install it | Transport `none` refuses the install (W12); the lease | 2 minutes |
| 13 | A renewal hangs (in `sudo`, the directory service, or the helper's 10-s lock wait and 10-s thermal read) | `run_with_timeout` kills the helper call after 5 s, and the renewal process lets go of the main lock; were the process itself stuck, its own 8-s limit kills it (row 33); the next pass tries again; otherwise the lease. A killed `sudo` leaves the helper to finish under its own lock, which orders it before any stop or start | 2 minutes, provided the helper's own reads return: `extend`'s `pmset -g batt` has no time limit (awake-helper:408, 449); were it to hang, the timer's `finish_session` would give up on the helper's lock after 10 s and the guard would retry about every 12 s until that `extend` let go (awake-helper:1340-1345) |
| 14 | A renewal races the user's stop | The main lock orders them. A stop that comes first leaves another token, or none, so no renewal; a renewal that comes first is followed by the stop, which ends the session at once. Without the lock a stale renewal stretched the user's next session from 5 to 30 s (probe-lease.out, step 6) | The stop's own time, about 1 s |
| 15 | A renewal races the helper's own end (`timeout` or a guardrail) | The helper's lock orders them; `finish_session` checks the end again under it (awake-helper:808-814), and an `extend` after `finish_session` exits 5. Before that, `extend` checks only that the timer or guard lives, not whether the end has passed (awake-helper:1072-1076): with the timer stopped, a renewal 5 s after a 3-s end brought the session back (review probe `probe-end.out`, section 3). So the renewal refuses a session whose `deadline_at` is at or before now, and counts it as ended (W29): an ended session never comes back | None |
| 16 | A renewal holds the main lock while the user acts | The renewal holds it at most about 8 s (its process's limit: 1 s to take the lock, the 5-s helper call, the rest); a user's command waits up to about 10 s (probe-race.out, step 2) | No failure |
| 17 | `awake --passwordless on\|off` holds the main lock during its password dialog (7183-7193, 4700-4716) | No renewal can run meanwhile. A dialog left open more than 90 s ends a lid-closed automatic session by the lease, with the lid open as the user types, so nothing sleeps; the watcher starts a new one once the lock is free | 2 minutes, harmless |
| 18 | The hooks fall silent: turned off, not trusted, a settings error, an agent's bug | The holder rules end the holders, then W6. The watcher is healthy and the lease does not shorten this; the freshness gate bounds it | 15 minutes (the idle limit), or the 2-hour quiet limit while Claude Code's last look reads `busy`; at most 2 hours plus 2 minutes |
| 19 | The agent crashes or is killed | Its holder is dropped (`kill -0` every pass, the start time every 10 s, 5.8), then W6. Where the recorded process is an editor or a daemon that lives on, the idle limit | About 2 to 4 s; 15 minutes for an editor's or daemon's process |
| 20 | A hook is killed, or hangs reading stdin | Its event is lost or never written; it holds nothing, and a hook killed while it holds `agent-lock/` is taken over after 5 s (5.8). The holders follow their time rules | No effect on the end |
| 21 | `agent-events` is removed, replaced or turned into a symlink | The log gate: the watcher ends its session (W6) and exits (W29); the next starting hook starts a new watcher. Otherwise the lease | One pass and the end; 2 minutes at most (probe: 4.04 s after the stand-in's log was removed, with a 4-second lease) |
| 22 | The runtime folder is removed (a `/tmp` cleanup, an `rm`) | `agent-events` is gone with it, so the log gate: the watcher tries to end its session (W6) and exits. Otherwise the renewal cannot take the main lock, whose folder is gone, so the lease ends the session. A lid-open runner exits after 3 passes without its state file (5957-5965). The next starting hook makes the folder again and starts one watcher | 2 minutes; about 3 s lid-open |
| 23 | The disk is full | Hooks cannot append, and `extend` cannot rewrite its record (`write_kv_file`, awake-helper:210-223), so the lease. The end then needs `pmset` to restore the settings: if it fails, `finish_session` records `failed` and does not sleep the Mac (awake-helper:829-832, 850-856), and `SleepDisabled` may stay 1 with no session, as for any session today. Whether `pmset` fails on a full disk was not checked | 2 minutes if `pmset` still applies the settings; otherwise not bounded, as for any session (not verified) |
| 24 | Awake is updated during a run | The running watcher keeps the functions it parsed. With the lid open a new-version hook replaces it (row 6). With the lid closed it runs on; if the update broke something its renewals need, the lease. A future helper protocol makes `extend` exit 6 (row 12) | About 3 s lid-open; 2 minutes lid-closed |
| 25 | The clock is set forward by X | If X is more than the time left, an early `timeout`: a false end, which sleeps a closed Mac, the safe direction | At once |
| 26 | The clock is set back by X | The timer and the runner count wall-clock time (`now=start_epoch+SECONDS`, awake-helper:1323-1334; 5940-5956), and so do their scheduled checks, so the end and the checks of the watched PID come up to X later; the same holds for any session today. How macOS bash 3.2's `SECONDS` follows a clock change is checked in Mac QA (W.6 item 37). For a healthy watcher, see row 37 | 2 minutes plus X for a hung watcher; about 2 s plus X for a dead one |
| 27 | The Mac sleeps during a lid-open automatic session (lid closed, the Apple menu) | Asleep is asleep. On wake the runner ends a passed end at once; a watcher with working holders starts a new session within a pass, with the lid open | About 1 s after the wake |
| 28 | A lid-closed automatic session is put to sleep anyway | `SleepDisabled` 1 refuses every sleep (awake-helper:818-820); if one happened, the timer ends a passed end right after the wake (awake-helper:1320-1322) and sleeps a closed Mac | About 1 s after the wake |
| 29 | Logout | The watcher dies with the user's processes, so the PID tie, provided the helper's timer and guard, root processes started through `sudo` with `nohup` (awake-helper:872-886), outlive the logout; that was not checked, and it is the same for every lid-closed session today (W.6 item 38 checks it). A lid-open session's runner dies, and `caffeinate -w` lets go of the assertion at once (5919-5934) | About 2 s |
| 30 | Restart, shutdown or power loss | Nothing runs; the helper's boot-restore restores the settings at the next boot (awake-helper:1217-1254) | Until the next boot, with the Mac off |
| 31 | The helper's timer is killed or hangs | The guard, every 2 s: at the end, once the timer is gone or its heartbeat is more than 60 s old (awake-helper:1418-1520) | The end plus about 2 s if killed; the later of the end and the last heartbeat plus about 62 s if hung |
| 32 | The timer and the guard both killed (only root can) | Nothing, until `awake --stop` restores a stale record (7425-7463) or boot-restore runs, as for any session today | Not bounded |
| 33 | A renewal or an explicit end hangs inside the main lock or the lid-open deadline lock (a stuck `ps`, `mktemp`, `mv`, `stat` or `date`) | Its own time limit (8 s for a renewal, 60 s for an end) kills its process; the locks then name a dead process, which `acquire_main_lock` and `acquire_deadline_lock` take over at once (1340-1354, 5671-5672), so the runner ends a lid-open session at its end and a user's command goes through; otherwise the lease. In a subshell of the watcher, as the third revision had it, the locks named the live watcher: a lid-open session ran on past its end and a user's `--stop` took 32.6 s (review probe `probe-end2.out`). Residual: the watcher's whole process group stopped (`kill -STOP -<group>`, a debugger) while a renewal holds a lock; then the lid-open runner waits and user commands fail after 10 s, while a lid-closed session still ends by the helper's timer, and a lid-open session never keeps a closed Mac awake | The lease plus 8 s |
| 34 | Two watchers: `agent-watch` alone is removed, and a hook starts a second one | A watcher rewrites `agent-watch` only under `agent-lock/` and only while it is missing or names itself; one that finds another live watcher named there ends its own session (W6) and exits (W4). Each session stays bounded by its own token, PID tie and lease meanwhile | One pass and an end; 2 minutes at most |
| 35 | A hook flood (a program appends thousands of lines) | The watcher reads at most 2000 lines a pass, so its renewals and its beat keep their pace (5.6); if a pass still runs long, the lease may end the session early, the safe direction, and a stuck beat gets the watcher replaced (W27). The 128-holder cap bounds its arrays | No late end; at worst an early one |
| 36 | The agent itself never stops: a `/loop` whose turns start within the 60-s grace, a `/goal` or a Stop hook that always continues, a retry loop that calls a tool every few seconds | Nothing fails: the holder rules read real work, so a healthy watcher renews. The presence limit (B-18 (a)): no renewal once nobody has used the Mac for 24 hours; then the lease, and a closed Mac sleeps. Also the guardrails, the user, and the 365-day outer limit | 24 hours after the last input at the Mac, plus 2 minutes; up to 365 days without B-18 (a) |
| 37 | The clock is set back by X while holders are recent (a manual change, `timed` correcting a wrong clock, a VM restore) | Event times are clamped to the watcher's clock, and a pass whose `now` is more than 5 s before the previous one clamps every stored time to `now` (5.6), so each holder gets one fresh idle limit and the freshness gate counts from the clamp. With the lid open, a session whose end is now more than the lease ahead (set before the step) is replaced in the same pass, as W28 does; with the lid closed, row 26's helper-side residual remains | 15 minutes plus 2 minutes for the holders; row 26 for the session's own end |
| 38 | The password-free check fails once (a `sudo -n` cut off by its limit under load) | Nothing: no check runs while renewals succeed, a failed renewal is retried every pass, and W28's switch to lid-open needs two failures for want of a password at least 30 s apart (W29) | No false end |
| 39 | A start by hand replaces a lid-closed automatic session with the lid closed, and the new start fails (a helper error, a race with the battery, a watched process that exits first) | The handover checked the start's refusals before stopping anything; when the start fails anyway, `main` starts a 1-second lid-closed session through transport `none`, which ends as `timeout`, so the helper sleeps the closed Mac (5.9). Residual: that start refused too (password-free mode turned off a moment before): the Mac stays awake with no session until the lid opens and closes or idle sleep applies, as for a failed start after any stop today (awake-helper:621-624) | About 2 s; residual not bounded |
| 40 | `--ai-hooks off`, for all agents or for one, with the lid closed (over SSH, from an agent's shell tool, by the uninstaller) | The automatic session ends through W6's token-checked `command-finished` before the watcher gets TERM, as `process_exited`, which sleeps a closed Mac and changes nothing with the lid open (K11); the third revision's stop request would have ended it as `stopped`, which does not sleep it | About 1 s |
| 41 | The user stops an automatic session whose watcher has just died | The stop writes the pause because the session is automatic (`agent=auto`), whether a watcher lives or not (5.9), so the next hook's new watcher starts nothing; the session ends as `stopped` | About 1 s |
| 42 | The floor: the work ends | The renewals stop; the session ends at its last end, as `timeout`, and a closed Mac sleeps | At the later of the user's own end and 2 minutes after the last renewal: its own end when the work ended more than 90 s before it, otherwise 90 to 120 s after the last turn's end |
| 43 | The floor: the watcher dies, hangs or freezes | The session is not tied to the watcher, so the lease alone: nothing renews it, and it ends at its last end | 2 minutes |
| 44 | The floor: password-free mode is turned off, or the feature or the agent is turned off | No renewal (no prompt); the session ends at its last end | At its own end if no renewal ran; otherwise within 2 minutes of the last renewal |
| 45 | The floor: the user stops the session | `stopped`, at once, with the stop pause | About 1 s |

The 365 days: the helper bounds each end to 365 days from now (awake-helper:1115-1132), not a session's total length, so a session renewed every 30 s has no total limit in root; the watcher renews nothing past 365 days after the session's start (W29). Adrafinil's bounds for comparison: its hold time limits (1 to 24 hours), its CPU sweep and its 24-hour backstop all run in its user-level daemon, and its root helper's only check of its own clears the block 60 s after the daemon's last connection drops (section 1). Its rows 2, 3 and 4 would be bounded by nothing in root. Its 24-hour backstop does bound row 36, counted from a hold's first acquire whatever happens (IdleReleaseEvaluator.swift:27-40); B-18 (a) bounds the same row by 24 hours without input at the Mac, which leaves a user who is at the Mac uninterrupted, and B-18 (c) is Adrafinil's form.

## 6. Worked example: the owner's setup

The owner: "I currently use Cursor as my editor and run Claude Extension inside it." Cursor's own agent is there too. This is the first setup that must work, and section 10.2 checks it before any code.

### 6.1 What gets installed

The owner turns on `Keep awake while AI works` in the menu, or runs `awake --ai-hooks on`. With password-free mode off, the app first offers to turn it on (M3), and the CLI refuses with the message of K8 (H-2 (a)).

1. Awake finds `~/.claude` (Claude Code, also used by the extension) and `~/.cursor` with `/Applications/Cursor.app` (Cursor). It finds no `~/.codex` and no `~/.gemini`, so it says that Codex and Gemini CLI are not found and changes nothing for them (H-6).
2. It adds 13 handlers to `~/.claude/settings.json` (K.3.1), leaving the owner's own hooks as they are. For example, under `UserPromptSubmit`:
   ```json
   {"type": "command", "command": "'/Users/<you>/Library/Application Support/Awake/bin/awake' --agent-hook claude turn-start >/dev/null 2>&1; exit 0", "timeout": 10}
   ```
   The extension reads this file, "shared between the extension and CLI" (vs-code.md:525), and applies the change to running conversations (settings.md:584; section 10 checks it in the panel).
3. It adds 7 entries to `~/.cursor/hooks.json` (K.3.3), creating the file with `"version": 1` if it is missing. For example:
   ```json
   {"version": 1, "hooks": {"beforeSubmitPrompt": [{"command": "'/Users/<you>/Library/Application Support/Awake/bin/awake' --agent-hook cursor turn-start >/dev/null 2>&1; exit 0", "timeout": 10}], "…": []}}
   ```
   Cursor reloads the file when it changes ([CD] hooks.md:171); one project needed a new conversation before the hooks ran ([RO] cursor.md:664-666).
4. It stores the lid mode and prints, per agent, what it did:
   ```
   Claude Code: on (13 handlers in ~/.claude/settings.json)
   Cursor: on (7 entries in ~/.cursor/hooks.json)
   Codex: not found
   Gemini CLI: not found
   Automatic sessions: lid-closed (password-free mode is on)
   ```

### 6.2 What fires

| The owner does | What runs | Events Awake logs | What happens |
|---|---|---|---|
| Types a prompt in the Claude Code panel | The panel's own `claude` process (one per conversation, vs-code.md:217) runs `UserPromptSubmit` | `turn-start claude:s:<uuid>` | The hook starts a watcher; the watcher starts an automatic session (lid open), a 2-minute lease it renews every 30 s while Claude works |
| Claude runs tools | `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse`, async | `tool-start`, `working` | The holder stays working |
| Claude asks for permission | `PermissionRequest` (and `Notification` `permission_prompt` 6 s later, which Awake does not hook) | `waiting` | Up to 10 minutes, then the last look (B-1) |
| Claude finishes | `Stop` | `turn-end` | 60 s grace, then the last look, then the session ends; a closed Mac sleeps |
| Presses Stop or Esc in the panel | Nothing: no hook, and no `idle_prompt` in the panel (2.2) | none | The holder stays working until the 15-minute idle limit, then the last look reads `idle` and the session ends |
| Types a prompt in Cursor's Agent chat | Cursor runs `beforeSubmitPrompt` from `~/.cursor/hooks.json`, and also Awake's Claude Code handler from `~/.claude/settings.json` (H-5) | `turn-start cursor:s:<conversation_id>`; the Claude Code handler sees `conversation_id` (or Cursor's environment without a Claude Code session id) and writes nothing | One holder for the Cursor conversation; the same automatic session serves both agents |
| Cursor's agent thinks and runs tools | `afterAgentThought`, `preToolUse`, `postToolUse`; with the third-party import on, also Awake's Claude Code `PreToolUse` and `PostToolUse` handlers, which stand down | `tool-start`, `working` | The holder stays working; each tool call waits for two short Awake processes (H-5) |
| Cursor's agent shows an approval card | Nothing ([FL]:10-13) | none | It counts as a running tool, up to the 15-minute idle limit |
| Presses Stop in Cursor's agent | `stop` with `aborted` | `turn-end` | 60 s grace, then the end (no last look for Cursor) |
| Closes the Cursor window | The SDK ends the panel's `claude` (2.2); Cursor runs `sessionEnd` with `window_close` | `session-end cursor:…`; the Claude holder's process is gone | Both holders end; the session ends after the grace |
| Reloads the window | The panel's `claude` restarts with the same session id, and continues an interrupted step by itself (vs-code.md:291-300) | `tool-start claude:s:<uuid>` from the new process | The holder follows the new process (5.7) |
| Runs `claude` in Cursor's integrated terminal | The standalone CLI, a terminal session (2.1); its hooks have `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID`, so W25 never takes them for Cursor's, whatever of Cursor's environment they inherit (checked in 10.2 (z), which this surface ships after) | as in the panel, plus `idle` from `idle_prompt` | As in the panel, but an interrupt ends sooner |
| Clicks the Awake icon while Claude works | The stop writes `agent-pause` before the session ends (5.9) | none | `Awake is off`; the pause stands for both agents until the owner types the next prompt in either, with the lid open; Claude's next tool calls start nothing (B-5) |
| Clicks `Keep awake for 1 hour` while Claude works | `awake --start --duration-seconds 3600` through the handover | none | The owner's own hour replaces the automatic session; it lasts the hour, or longer while Claude or Cursor works (B-14) |
| Starts a 30-minute session from the menu, gives Claude a long task and closes the lid | The owner's own session; the floor (5.9) | the usual events | From 90 s before the 30 minutes, the watcher renews the owner's session every 30 s while a holder works; it ends 90 to 120 s after the last reply, or at 30 minutes if the work ended sooner, and the Mac sleeps |
| Runs `awake -w <PID of a build>` and closes the lid while Claude works | The owner's session tied to the build | the usual events | When the build exits, the session ends and the Mac sleeps, although Claude still works: a session tied to a process is never kept by the floor (5.9) |
| The watcher hangs or is frozen while Claude works with the lid closed | Nothing renews the lease | none | The helper ends the session within 2 minutes and sleeps the Mac (5.14) |

The work is the same whether the conversation sits in the sidebar, in an editor tab or in a separate window, as each runs its own `claude` process.

### 6.3 What the owner sees

- The menu: `Keep awake while AI works`, checked. During a pause also `Paused until your next prompt` and `Resume now`.
- The tooltip and the menu's first line: `Awake is on while Claude works.`, `Awake is on while Cursor works.`, or `Awake is on while Claude and Cursor work.`, and the waiting and ending sentences of 5.11; during a session the owner timed, `Awake is on and has 12 minutes left, or longer while Claude works.`
- Settings, `AI agents`: Claude Code on, with `~/.claude/settings.json`; Cursor on, with `~/.cursor/hooks.json`; Codex and Gemini CLI "Not found"; the lid mode; and any note or pause.
- In the agents themselves, nothing: the hooks print nothing, so no message appears in Claude's panel or in Cursor's chat. The extension's Hooks dialog lists Awake's handlers among the owner's (vs-code.md:132), and the owner can delete them there too; `awake --ai-hooks status` then reads `partial`.

### 6.4 What does not work in this setup, and what to do

- An interrupt in the Claude Code panel keeps the Mac awake up to 15 minutes (above). B-7 (c) would shorten it, at a cost.
- An approval card of Cursor's agent left open keeps it awake up to 15 minutes, and then lets it sleep while the card waits.
- A Remote-SSH, WSL or container window runs Claude Code on the remote host (inferred: VS Code runs workspace extensions there; cc-changelog.md:1977), so its hooks never reach the Mac; for Cursor's agent in such a window, where user hooks run was not found (Cursor only names a variable for remote workspaces, [CD] hooks.md:1463). If the connection itself should survive, `awake -w <Cursor's PID>` or a timed session covers it.
- Cursor's `agent -p` and Cursor's Remote Control and My Machines run no user hooks: `awake -- agent -p "<task>"`, Cursor's own "Keep this computer awake", or `awake -w <worker PID>` (2.1).
- If the owner installs Codex or Gemini CLI later, `Settings` lists it as found and off (H-6), provided the release offers that agent, that is, its preflight passed (S-4). Codex's IDE extension in Cursor is not offered until 10.4 shows Awake's trusted hooks firing there, and the README lists it as not verified; Gemini Code Assist is not supported (2.5).
- Cursor's local agents in the Agents Window are not offered until 10.2 (y) has run them, and Cmd+K only once 10.2 (x) has (2.1).
- With the lid closed, a session the owner tied to a process (`-w`, `--`) sleeps the Mac when the process exits, even while an agent works; a timed session does not (5.9).
- Whether the last look lists the panel's sessions, and whether the panel runs every hook it should, is checked in section 10.2. A panel holder that a working last look does not list is marked `unlisted` and follows B-7 (b)'s timings (5.6), whose 3-minute grace the lease now keeps to its end (5.4), so this holds even if only some sessions are listed, or none.
- Whether Cursor's subagents have their own conversation ids is checked in 10.2 (t); until then a turn that used one may keep a closed Mac awake up to the idle limit (2.4).
- With Cursor's third-party import on, each Cursor tool call also runs Awake's Claude Code `PreToolUse` handler synchronously (H-5). To roll back a Cursor problem, turn off both: `awake --ai-hooks off --agent cursor --agent claude`, or Cursor Settings → Agents → Third-Party Imports (K.9).

## 7. W: automatic sessions in the CLI

### W.1 Today

See section 3. In short: sessions tied to one process (`-w`, `--`), with an end through `command-finished`, and timed sessions whose end the helper's `extend` can move later. There is nothing for an agent's turns, no way to start a lid-closed session that is sure not to prompt, no way to renew a session that is sure to touch only the right one (`extend` has no token), and every call parses all of `bin/awake`.

### W.2 Decisions

| # | Decision | Choice and why | Rejected, and why |
|---|---|---|---|
| W1 | What starts and ends the holding | Each agent's hooks, through its adapter (2, 5.2), with the agent process's exit, the time limits and, for Claude Code, the last look as safety nets (5.5-5.8), and the lease as the guarantee that root ends the session when the watcher stops renewing it (W29). | Polling (O6, O7) and heuristics (O10): undocumented, in research preview, or wrong while the agent thinks, and they need a process that always runs. A lease renewed by the hooks themselves (O2): a helper run per hook event, a session that ends in the middle of a long tool call, as hooks fire only at events, and no token, so it extends whatever session runs. W29's lease is renewed by the watcher on a clock and checked by token, which answers all three. |
| W2 | Holder keys and the payload | The key is `AGENT:s:<id>` or `AGENT:a:<id>`, with `AGENT` from the handler's command and each id matching `^[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,128}$`. Anything else is dropped at the hook. The hook cuts the payload at the first content key of any agent (`"prompt"`, `"tool_input"`, `"tool_response"`, `"tool_output"`, `"last_assistant_message"`, `"text"`, `"attachments"`, `"prompt_response"`, `"message"`, `"details"`, `"llm_request"`) and looks for its ids only before it, with patterns such as `"agent_id"[[:space:]]*:[[:space:]]*"([A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,128})"` kept in variables (`[[ $s =~ $re ]]`, as at 4067). Per agent: Claude Code and Codex take `session_id`, `agent_id` and `tool_name`; Cursor takes `conversation_id`, which it sends first, as its `session_id` comes after the content ([RO] cursor-logs.txt:61); Gemini CLI takes `session_id`, its first key (hookEventHandler.ts:371-385). The fallbacks `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID` and `GEMINI_SESSION_ID` are used only when stdin has no id. A Claude Code `turn-end` also looks for `"background_tasks"[[:space:]]*:[[:space:]]*\[[[:space:]]*\{` in the whole payload: the key comes after `last_assistant_message` (hooks.md:2577), a `Stop` carries no tool input or output, and every quote inside its strings is escaped, so an unescaped match is that key. If 10.2 (t) shows that Cursor's subagents have their own ids, Cursor's `subagentStop` takes `child_conversation_id` instead of `conversation_id`, one more entry in Cursor's table (5.7). Section 10 checks the key order of each agent. | The variable first: Claude Code's "is updated on `/clear`" (env-vars.md:371), so a `SessionEnd` might name the new session, while stdin carries the retiring one (ClaudeCodeIntegration.swift:93-96). A pattern anchored at the payload's start that allows no `{` before the key: `effort`, an object, is a common field of exactly the tool events that need `agent_id` (hooks.md:736). The first match anywhere: a main-thread event would be filed under an `agent_id` from a tool's output. Cursor's `session_id`: it comes after the prompt. |
| W3 | How hooks talk to the watcher | One append-only file, `agent-events`, mode 600, in the user's runtime folder. Each line is `VERB KEY EPOCH AGENT_PID BG RC`, in ASCII, with the agent in `KEY`. The watcher stores each event's time as `min(EPOCH, now of the pass that read it)`, uses the hook's `EPOCH` only to order events after that clamp, and clamps every stored time to `now` when a pass's `now` is more than 5 s before the previous pass's, so a clock set back cannot keep holders or the freshness gate alive for the size of the jump (5.6). It reads at most 2000 lines a pass (5.6). The protocol: (1) every hook appends first, with one `printf >>`, which for a line this short is a single `write` with `O_APPEND`, and checks the watcher after; (2) the watcher reads new lines through a descriptor it keeps open (`exec 4<`), with `LC_ALL=C` and builtins; a line not yet ended by a newline is consumed by `read` (probe), so the watcher keeps the piece and puts it in front of the next one; (3) on every pass it saves the number of complete lines it has read in `agent-offset`, and a new watcher skips that many lines and drops events older than the idle limit by their time (bash 3.2 cannot report a byte offset); (4) it exits only under `agent-lock/`: it removes `agent-watch`, reads to the end once more, and if a line arrived it writes `agent-watch` again and goes on; otherwise it saves the line count it has read, releases the lock and exits, leaving the file as it is, so that a line a hook appends after that last read is read by the next watcher, which skips only the saved count; (5) past 64 KB, under `agent-lock/`, it rotates with no moment at which the file is missing: it creates `agent-events.new` (mode 600), hard-links `agent-events` to `agent-events.1`, renames `agent-events.new` over `agent-events` (`mv -f`, atomic), waits one pass for a hook that opened the old file just before, reads `.1` to its end through descriptor 4, which still holds the old file, deletes `.1`, and opens descriptor 4 on the new file; `agent-offset` names the file its count belongs to, so a watcher killed during a rotation resumes in `.1` first. (6) When `main` starts a watcher, it appends the starting event again, whether the fast path wrote it or not: a duplicate changes nothing, and a starting event lost in a race still reaches the new watcher. Lines with an unknown verb, agent or key, or bad numbers are skipped, so a newer `awake`'s hooks cannot confuse an older watcher. The watcher also keeps the newest event time it has read, for W29's freshness gate, and checks on each pass, outside a rotation, that `agent-events` is still the file descriptor 4 reads (W29's log gate). | One file per holder, with modification times: bash 3.2 has no builtin for a file's time, and `-nt` compares whole seconds. A socket or FIFO to a watcher: a hook would block or fail while no watcher reads. Appending only after seeing a live watcher: a watcher that exits just then never reads the event. One file per agent: one watcher reads them all anyway. Emptying the file at exit, and rotating through `mv` and a new empty file, as an earlier revision did: hooks append without `agent-lock/`, so a line written after the last read, or into a file created or truncated mid-rotation, was lost, and a lost `turn-start` left the new watcher with no holder. |
| W4 | The watcher | An internal mode, `awake --agent-watch`, started with `start_detached --agent-watch >/dev/null` (3567-3573) through `/usr/bin/env -i` and a short list of variables (5.8), so that it never carries one hook's agent environment for its whole life: its own process group, under `nohup`, so a hook's end, or an agent killing an async hook at teardown (hooks.md:3732-3735; codex-rs/hooks/src/engine/command_runner.rs:188-196), does not end it, and the PID that `start_detached` prints does not reach any agent's context. It runs `cd /` first, so it never holds a project folder. `agent-lock/` lets only one starter at a time decide; it has the main lock's scheme, with the holder's PID and start time in `agent-lock/pid` and a takeover of a stale lock, through `main_lock_is_abandoned` and `take_over_stale_main_lock` with the lock folder as a parameter (1377-1413). A starter waits up to about 1 s for it. The watcher records `pid=`, `started=`, `version=` and `beat=` in `agent-watch` and rewrites it on every pass, under `agent-lock/`, only while the file is missing or names itself; when it names another live watcher (`process_is_same`), the older watcher ends its own session through W6 and exits, so a removed `agent-watch` never leaves two watchers for long (5.8). A watcher of another version is replaced while the lid is open (5.8). A pass every 2 s (0.5 s in the dry run). | The caller's environment, as `start_detached` passes it today: one hook's agent variables and `PATH` for the watcher's whole life (5.8). A LaunchAgent that always runs, like Adrafinil's: install and uninstall steps, a process all the time, and no dry-run test. The hook itself waiting for the work: a hook has to end within milliseconds. A lock without a holder record: a hook killed at its timeout would leave it for good. |
| W5 | How a session follows the watcher | The watcher runs `awake --agent-session -w $$ --duration-seconds 120 …` as a child (5.3), the path that `awake -w PID --duration-seconds N` uses for any process, and renews it with the helper's existing `extend` or the Caffeine state file (W29). Nothing new in the helper or the runner. | A new helper command for automatic sessions, or a helper-side lease: protocol 10, a password prompt for every user at the update (4160-4172), for what `extend` already does. |
| W6 | How a session ends | `agent_end_session` runs as its own process, `awake --agent-end TOKEN`, which the watcher starts with its PID and start time under `run_with_timeout` 60 s (2339-2363), and which, like `--caffeinate-start`, runs no `require_macos`: it takes the main lock (`acquire_main_lock` waits up to 10 s, and exits on failure, so the watcher simply tries again on its next pass, 1416-1463); it counts the session as its own only when the running record (the helper's session file, or the state file for lid-open) has the token `TOKEN`, `watch_pid` equal to the watcher's PID, `watch_started` equal to its start time, and the user's `uid`; and it passes that record's token to `request_helper_session_stop command-finished TOKEN`, a new optional argument that makes it return 1 without writing anything when the current token differs. For lid-open it makes the same check before writing `command-finished` with the token and calling `request_active_session_stop`. The reason is `process_exited`, so a closed Mac sleeps (awake-helper:850-855). `run_bound_command` (4505-4508) can pass its token too, which closes the same small window for `awake --`. The same process ends the session for `--ai-hooks off` (K11), for a settings change (W28), for a clock set back with the lid open (5.6) and when a second watcher is found (W4). | A subshell of the watcher, as the third revision had it: the main lock and the deadline lock would name the live watcher (`$$`), so an end stuck inside them would hold up the user's commands and the lid-open runner for as long as the watcher lives (W29). Ending without the lock: a stop and start by the user just before the request would end the user's new session as `process_exited` and sleep a closed Mac, as the helper never reads the token in `command-finished` (awake-helper:39-46, 1353-1356), against the principle. Reading the token after the start: it can adopt a session the user just started. A stop request: `stopped` never sleeps a closed Mac (awake-helper:847-849). A new reason, such as `agent_idle`: the lid-open stop waiter accepts only the known reasons (3811), the CLI's and the app's texts need it, and lid-closed sessions need a helper change. |
| W7 | Timings | End grace 60 s, idle limit 15 min, waiting 10 min, background 1 h, quiet limit 2 h (also the freshness gate, W29), the lease 120 s renewed at 90 s or less left (B-15), a renewal's main-lock try 1 s, helper call 5 s and process limit 8 s, an end process's limit 60 s, the presence limit 24 h (B-18), 2000 lines read per pass, the clock-step threshold 5 s (5.6), the outer limit `MAX_DURATION_SECONDS` after the session's start (365 days, 121-122), the 2-second rule, `idle`'s 50 s, the input windows of W26 (5 s, and 60 s for Codex, with input at least 2 s after the stop), a stuck watcher's 90 s and the 5 s between its two looks, retry 60 s, guardrail and settings checks 30 s, the beat's 10 s, rotation at 64 KB. As constants after `WATCH_IDENTITY_CHECK_SECONDS` (165). In the dry run: grace 2 s, idle 5 s, waiting 3 s, background 5 s, quiet 6 s, stuck 5 s with 1 s between the looks, settings checks 1 s, the lease from `AWAKE_TEST_AGENT_LEASE_SECONDS` (8 s by default there) renewed at 6 s or less left, the renewal's helper call 2 s and process limit 4 s, the last look's limit 1 s, the presence limit from `AWAKE_TEST_AGENT_AWAY_SECONDS`, B-7 (b)'s grace 10 s and waiting 6 s, the outer limit from `AWAKE_TEST_AGENT_LIMIT_SECONDS`, 1 s for the 2-second rule, 2 s for `idle`, retry 1 s, checks 1 s, beat 3 s, rotation from `AWAKE_TEST_AGENT_ROTATE_BYTES`; the `AWAKE_TEST_*` values are read only in the dry run. Without the last look (B-7 (b)), for Claude Code: waiting 20 min and grace 3 min. The same values for every agent (B-13). At its start the watcher checks that the constants in use keep the order of B-15: renewal threshold − pass > grace + last look's limit + pass (real: 88 > 67 s; dry run: 5.5 > 3.5 s); a dry-run run whose `AWAKE_TEST_*` values break it logs that and exits, so a test cannot pass on timing luck. B-7 (b)'s longer grace is exempt, as its holders renew up to their grace's end (5.4). The dry-run lease is 8 s rather than 4 s for that order: with 4 s renewed at 3 s left, the end could be only 2.5 s ahead when the work ends, less than the 2-s grace plus a pass, the last look's stand-in and the main lock, so the lease could end the session before W6 did and checks expecting `process_exited` would be flaky. (B-1, B-2, B-12, B-15, B-18.) | Settings in the app: more UI, for values few users would change. The first version's 12 h: the owner's answer (B-2). A lease of 60 s or less: the session could time out during the last look at the end of the grace, and with the lid closed B-11 would forbid a new one (B-15). |
| W8 | Waiting | 5.6. Claude Code: `PermissionRequest` and `PreToolUse` with matcher `AskUserQuestion\|ExitPlanMode`. Codex: `PreToolUse` with matcher `request_user_input`; its `PermissionRequest` is a `tool-start`, as it also fires before the automatic reviewer (B-1). Gemini CLI: `Notification`. Cursor: none (2.4). Two files, as two different processes write them. **The stop pause**, `agent-pause` (`since=<epoch>`), lives in Awake's own folder `~/Library/Application Support/Awake/` (the dry-run runtime folder in the dry run), mode 600, so that it survives a restart and macOS's 3-day cleanup of `/tmp` (166-168), as Adrafinil's "Let it sleep" does (Models/PersistedDaemonState.swift:3-6). Main's stop paths write it under the main lock before the stop request (5.9); `main`'s hook path removes it under `agent-lock/` when W26's test passes; `--ai-hooks resume` and `--ai-hooks off` remove it. Nothing else does, a start by hand included. The fast path tests it with builtins (`-f`, `! -L`, `-O`). Main's stop paths write it whenever the session being stopped is automatic, with or without a live watcher (5.9). **The guardrail pause**, `agent-guard` (`reason=low_battery\|overheated\|unplugged`, `since=`), lives in the runtime folder; only the watcher writes and removes it, when the cause has gone, and it needs to outlive no restart, as a new start checks the guardrails anyway. An `--agent-session` start refuses under the main lock while either file exists, so the watcher can never start a session past a pause written a moment before. Hooks append as usual during a pause; the watcher starts nothing while one stands. During a stop pause a `turn-start` without a subagent id also goes on to `main`, which lifts the pause under W26's test. | Dropping events during the pause, as the first version did: `Resume now` would find no holders, and a prompt that lifts the pause would start a session only for its own agent. Clearing the holders and doing nothing more: the next tool call would start a session again. The watcher inferring the stop from the session's end record, as an earlier revision did: a pass that saw no session and a working holder could start a new session before it noticed the stop, a session started and stopped within one pass was never seen, and a later lid-open stop removes the status file that holds the record (6241). One file with both kinds: the stop's writer and the watcher would overwrite each other. A start by hand that clears the pause: an agent's own `awake -- npm test` would undo the user's stop, and a session started on battery after an `unplugged` end would let the next automatic session run on battery. The stop pause in the runtime folder: it would end at a restart or after 3 days without a watcher, and the next scheduled turn would start sessions without a typed prompt. |

On macOS (CI) the shipped JXA parser reads the JSON mock; the Linux emulation has no JXA (as for 12o) and uses a dry-run stand-in that reads only the `pid` and `status` pairs of the test's JSON. The lease, the outer limit and the presence limit come from `AWAKE_TEST_AGENT_LEASE_SECONDS`, `AWAKE_TEST_AGENT_LIMIT_SECONDS` and `AWAKE_TEST_AGENT_AWAY_SECONDS` (W7).
